A categorical account of composition methods in logic Thanks: Supported by EPSRC grant EP/T00696X/1: Resources and co-resources: a junction between categorical semantics and descriptive complexity. The main body of work was done while the first author was employed by the University of Cambridge. PubID: pubid: Accepted at LiCS 2023.
Tomáš Jakl, Dan Marsden, Nihil Shah, Department of Computer Science University of Oxford ORCID: 0000-0003-2844-0828

Abstract
We present a categorical theory of the composition methods in finite model theory – a key technique enabling modular reasoning about complex structures by building them out of simpler components. The crucial results required by the composition methods are Feferman–Vaught–Mostowski (FVM) type theorems, which characterize how logical equivalence behaves under composition and transformation of models. Our results are developed by extending the recently introduced game comonad semantics for model comparison games. This level of abstraction allow us to give conditions yielding FVM type results in a uniform way. Our theorems are parametric in the classes of models, logics and operations involved. Furthermore, they naturally account for the positive existential fragment, and extensions with counting quantifiers of these logics. We also reveal surprising connections between FVM type theorems, and classical concepts in the theory of monads. We illustrate our methods by recovering many classical theorems of practical interest, including a refinement of a previous result by Dawar, Severini, and Zapata concerning the 3-variable counting logic and cospectrality. To highlight the importance of our techniques being parametric in the logic of interest, we prove a family of FVM theorems for products of structures, uniformly in the logic in question, which cannot be done using specific game arguments.



I
Introduction

Composition methods
constitute a family of techniques in finite model theory for understanding the logical properties of complex structures
[
1
]
. One works in a modular fashion, building a structure out of simpler components. The larger structure can then be understood in terms of the logical properties of its parts, and how they behave under the operations used in the construction.

The first result of this type was proved by Mostowski
[
2
]
, who showed that the first-order theory of the product of two structures
A\times B
is determined by the first-order theories of
A
and
B
. Later, Feferman and Vaught famously proved a very general result for first-order logic, which included showing that the same holds true for infinite products and infinite disjoint unions of structures of the same type
[
3
]
. Since then many more Feferman--Vaught--Mostowski (FVM) theorems
1
1

1

The terser Feferman–Vaught type theorem is more common in the literature.
have been shown for various operations, logics and types of structures. These theorems have important applications in the theory of algorithms
[
4
]
, for example in the development of algorithmic meta-theorems such as Courcelle’s theorem
[
5
]
.

For our purposes, the typical form of an FVM theorem for a fixed
n
-ary operation
H
and logics
L_{1},\ldots,L_{n+1}
is as follows.
Given structures
A_{1},\dots,A_{n}
and
B_{1},\dots,B_{n}
,

\displaystyle\mbox{For all $i$,}\quad A_{i}\equiv_{L_{i}}B_{i},

(1)

\displaystyle\mbox{implies}\quad H(A_{1},\,\ldots,\,A_{n})\equiv_{L_{n+1}}H(B_{1},\,\ldots,\,B_{n}).

Here
\equiv_{L}
denotes equivalence with respect to the logic
L
.
Typically,
n
is either one or two. For example, writing
\equiv_{FO}
for equivalence in first-order logic, Mostowski’s result is given in the form of (
1
) as:

A_{1}\equiv_{FO}B_{1}\kern 5.0pt\mbox{and}\kern 5.0ptA_{2}\equiv_{FO}B_{2}\kern 5.0pt\mbox{implies}\kern 5.0ptA_{1}\times A_{2}\equiv_{FO}B_{1}\times B_{2}

A key tool in finite model theory for establishing that two structures are logically equivalent is that of a model comparison game.
Examples of such games are the Ehrenfeucht–Fraïssé
[
6
,
7
]
, pebbling
[
8
]
and bisimulation games
[
9
]
, establishing equivalence in fragments of first order and modal logic.
FVM theorems are typically proven using these games, building a winning strategy for the composite structure out of winning strategies for the components.

Despite their usefulness, working with model comparison games often requires intricate combinatorial arguments that have to be carefully adapted with even the slightest change of problem domain. To mitigate the difficulty with using game arguments directly, finite model theorists introduced higher-level methods such as locality arguments or 0–1 laws to establish model equivalences or inexpressibility.

The recently introduced
game comonad
framework
[
10
,
11
]

provides a new such tool. This provides a unifying formalism for reasoning about model comparison games, using categorical methods.
The definition of a comonad enables a transfer of results from the formally dual theory of monads, commonly encountered in the categorical semantics of computation and universal algebra
[
12
,
13
,
14
]
.
Game comonads are designed to encapsulate a particular model comparison game, and are the key abstraction allowing us to give uniform results, whilst avoiding making arguments specific to a particular logic or its corresponding game.

The early work on game comonads focused on capturing many important logics
[
15
,
16
,
17
]
, laying the foundations for further developments. More recent results recover classical theorems from finite model theory, as well as completely new results
[
18
,
19
,
20
,
21
]
. See
[
22
]
for a recent survey and
[
23
]
for an axiomatic formulation.

Until now, there has been no account of the composition method with the game comonad framework.
We close this gap by introducing a novel method for proving FVM theorems within the game comonad approach, giving results uniformly in the classes of structures, operations and logics involved.
Instantiating the abstract results for our example game comonads yields concrete FVM theorems with respect to three typical fragments of first-order logic:

1.

the positive existential fragment
, i.e. the fragment of first-order logic of formulas not using universal quantification or negations,

2.

counting logic
, i.e. the extension of first-order logic with counting quantifiers,

3.

full
first-order logic.

As is standard in finite model theory
[
1
,
24
]
, it is natural to grade these logics by a resource parameter, such as quantifier depth.
Correspondingly, the game comonadic approach to grading is to consider collections of comonads
\mathbb{C}_{k}
, indexed by a resource parameter
k
.
As a source of concrete examples we shall consider three particular game comonads,
{\mathbb{E}_{k}}
,
{\mathbb{P}_{k}}
and
{\mathbb{M}_{k}}
.
The pair
{\mathbb{E}_{k}}
and
{\mathbb{P}_{k}}
capture first-order logic, with their resource parameters being quantifier depth and variable count respectively. The comonad
{\mathbb{M}_{k}}
encapsulates modal logic up to modal depth
k
.

Conventionally, proving an FVM theorem involves cleverly combining several winning model-comparison game strategies to form a winning strategy on the composite structures.
In our game comonads approach, we only need to find a collection of morphisms of a specified form to obtain an FVM theorem for the positive existential logic.
This collection of morphisms is a formal witness to a combination of strategies.
Perhaps surprisingly, to witness equivalence in the counting logic, the same collection of morphisms only has to satisfy two additional axioms which coincide with the standard definition of Kleisli law
[
25
]
.

Although of practical interest, the FVM theorems in Sections
III
and
IV
are relatively straightforward to prove. We present them in detail for pedagogical reasons to develop the ideas gradually.
The abstract FVM theorem for the full fragment is more technically challenging. This theorem shows that two additional assumptions suffice to extend an FVM theorem from counting logic to the full logic.
We can rephrase these assumptions categorically as showing the operation lifts to a parametric relative right adjoint over an appropriate category.
Surprising connections arise with classical results in monad theory, generalizing the notion of bilinear map and its classifying tensor product from ordinary linear algebra
[
26
,
27
]
.

Our three running example game comonads
{\mathbb{E}_{k}},{\mathbb{P}_{k}},{\mathbb{M}_{k}}
are introduced in Section
II
, along with some necessary background on comonads.
Sections
III
,
IV
and
V
all follow the same pattern. We introduce the categorical structure abstracting logical equivalence with respect to the studied logical fragment, establish the corresponding categorical FVM theorem and illustrate this with concrete examples.
The examples in Section
IV
include a new refinement of a result by Dawar, Severini, and Zapata
[
28
]
, showing that equivalence in 3-variable counting logic without equality and with restricted conjunctions implies cospectrality.
Section
VI
develops FVM theorems for products of structures, uniformly in the logic of interest, greatly generalising Mostowski’s original result. Unlike our methods, which are parametric in the choice of game comonad, a typical game argument cannot be used to prove such a result, as the game is tied to a specific choice of logic.

In fact, the game comonads
{\mathbb{E}_{k}}
and
{\mathbb{P}_{k}}
naturally deal with first-order logic
without equality
. There is a standard and very flexible technique for incorporating equality, and other “enrichments” of logics
[
11
]
. This is introduced and related to FVM theorems in Section
VII
, and illustrated with further examples, including the handling of weak-bisimulation.

II
Preliminaries

II-A

Categories of Relational Structures

We assume the basic definitions of category theory, including categories, functors and natural transformations, as can be found in any standard introduction such as
[
29
]
or
[
30
]
. Background on comonads, and any less standard material is introduced as needed.

A
relational signature

\sigma
is a set of
relation symbols
, each with an associated strictly positive natural number
arity
.
A
\sigma
-structure
A
consists of a
universe
set, also denoted
A
, and for each relation symbol
R\in\sigma
of arity
n
, an
n
-ary relation
R^{A}
on
A
.
A
morphism of
\sigma
-structures

f\colon A\rightarrow B
is a function of type
A\rightarrow B
, preserving relations, in the sense that for
n
-ary
R\in\sigma

R^{A}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\quad\mbox{implies}\quad R^{B}(f(a_{1}),\ldots,f(a_{n})).

For a fixed
\sigma
,
\sigma
-structures and their morphisms form our main category of interest
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
. We will also have need of the category of pointed relational structures
\mathcal{R}_{*}(\sigma)
. Here the objects
(A,a)
are a
\sigma
-structure
A
with a distinguished element
a\in A
. The morphisms are
\sigma
-structure morphisms that also preserve the distinguished element.

II-B

Comonads

A
comonad in Kleisli form
on a category
\mathcal{C}
is a triple
(\mathbb{C},\varepsilon,(-)^{*})
where
\mathbb{C}
is an object map
\mathrm{obj}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathrm{obj}(\mathcal{C})
, the
counit

\varepsilon
is an
\mathrm{obj}({\mathcal{C}})
-indexed family of morphisms
\varepsilon_{A}\colon\mathbb{C}(A)\to A
, for
A\in\mathrm{obj}(\mathcal{C})
. Lastly, the operation
(-)^{*}
maps morphisms
f:\mathbb{C}(A)\rightarrow B
to their
coextension

f^{*}:\mathbb{C}(A)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}(B)
,
subject to the following equations:

\displaystyle(\varepsilon_{A})^{*}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{C}(A)},\quad\varepsilon_{B}\circ f^{*}=f,\quad(g\circ f^{*})^{*}=g^{*}\circ f^{*},

(2)

It is then standard
[
14
]
that
\mathbb{C}
extends to a functor, with the action on morphisms defined by
\mathbb{C}(f)=(f\circ\varepsilon_{A})^{*}
. Also, the counit is a natural transformation
\mathbb{C}\Rightarrow{I\mkern-1.0mud}
, from
\mathbb{C}
to the identity functor
{I\mkern-1.0mud}\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
and the morphisms
\delta_{A}:=\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{C}(A)}^{*}:\mathbb{C}(A)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{2}(A)
form a
comultiplication
natural transformation satisfying

\varepsilon\circ\delta=\mathrm{id}=\mathbb{C}(\varepsilon)\circ\delta
and
\delta\circ\delta=\mathbb{C}(\delta)\circ\delta
.

We now introduce the game comonads that we shall regularly refer to in our examples. Each is parameterized by a positive integer
k
. We shall write
A^{+}
for the set of non-empty words over
A
, and
A^{\leq k}
for its restriction to words of at most
k
elements. The first two comonads are defined over
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
:

Ehrenfeucht–Fraïssé comonad
{\mathbb{E}_{k}}
:

The universe of
{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(A)
is
A^{\leq k}
. The counit
\varepsilon_{A}
maps
[a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}]
to
a_{n}
.
R\in\sigma
an
n
-ary relation symbol,
R^{{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(A)}(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})
iff:

1.

the
s_{i}
are pairwise comparable in the prefix order on words, and

2.

R^{A}(\varepsilon_{A}(s_{1}),\ldots,\varepsilon_{A}(s_{n}))
.

The coextension of
h:{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(A)\rightarrow B
is

h^{*}([a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}])=[h([a_{1}]),\ldots,h([a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}])].

Pebbling comonad
{\mathbb{P}_{k}}
:

The universe of
{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(A)
is

(\{0,\ldots,k-1\}\times A)^{+}.

The counit
\varepsilon_{A}
maps
[(p_{1},a_{1}),\dots,(p_{n},a_{n})]
to
a_{n}
.
We refer to the first element in a pair
(p,a)
as a
pebble index
. For
n
-ary
R\in\sigma
,

R^{{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(A)}(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})
iff:

1.

The
s_{i}
are pairwise comparable in the prefix order on words.

2.

For
s_{i},s_{j}
with
s_{i}
a prefix word of
s_{j}
, the pebble index of the last pair of
s_{i}
does not reappear in the remaining elements of
s_{j}
.

3.

R^{A}(\varepsilon_{A}(s_{1}),\ldots,\varepsilon_{A}(s_{n}))
.

For
h\colon{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(A)\rightarrow B
, the coextension
h^{*}
acts on words as:

\displaystyle h^{*}([(p_{1},a_{1}),\ldots,(p_{n},a_{n})]):=

\displaystyle[(p_{1},h([a_{1}])),\ldots,(p_{n},h([a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}]))]

We say that a signature
\sigma
is a
modal signature
if it only has relational symbols of arity one or two.

Modal comonad
{\mathbb{M}_{k}}
:

Defined over
\mathcal{R}_{*}(\sigma)
for a modal signature
\sigma
, the universe of
{\mathbb{M}_{k}}(A,a_{0})
consists of paths in
A
starting from
a_{0}
, i.e. sequences

a_{0}\xrightarrow{R_{1}}a_{1}\xrightarrow{R_{2}}a_{2}\xrightarrow{R_{3}}\ldots\xrightarrow{R_{n}}a_{n}

such that
n\leq k
and, for every
i
,
R_{i}\in\sigma
and
R_{i}^{A}(a_{i-1},a_{i})
. The counit and coextension act as for
{\mathbb{E}_{k}}
.

Given a sequence
s^{\prime}=a_{0}\xrightarrow{R_{1}}\ldots\xrightarrow{R_{n-1}}a_{n-1}
and its extension
s
by
a_{n-1}\xrightarrow{R_{n}}a_{n}
in
{\mathbb{M}_{k}}(A,a_{0})
, then
R_{n}^{{\mathbb{M}_{k}}(A,a_{0})}(s^{\prime},s)
and
R_{n}^{{\mathbb{M}_{k}}(A,a_{0})}
only consists of such pairs. For a unary
P\in\sigma
,
P^{{\mathbb{M}_{k}}(A,a_{0})}(s)
iff
P^{A}(a_{n})
.

Theorem II.1

(
[
10
,
11
]
)
.

{\mathbb{E}_{k}}
,
{\mathbb{M}_{k}}
and
{\mathbb{P}_{k}}
are comonads in Kleisli form.

The intuition for each of these comonads is that they encode the moves within one structure during the corresponding model comparison game. We shall introduce their mathematical properties as needed in later sections. See
[
11
]
for detailed discussions of all three comonads.

III
FVM theorems for positive existential fragments

For a game comonad
\mathbb{C}
, we introduce the following two relations on structures:

•

A\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{C}}B
if there exists a morphism
\mathbb{C}(A)\to B
.

•

A\equiv_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{C}}B
if
A\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{C}}B
and
B\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{C}}A
.

It is a standard fact, formulated categorically in
[
11
]
, that
A\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}B
if and only if for every positive existential sentence
\varphi
of quantifier depth at most
k
,

A\models\varphi
implies
B\models\varphi
,
and therefore
A\equiv_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}B
if and only if structures
A
and
B
agree on the quantifier depth
k
fragment. Similarly,
A\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}B
and
A\equiv_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}B
correspond to the same relationships, but for the
k
variable fragment
[
10
]
.

Remark III.1
.

For conciseness, in this section, and Sections
IV
,
V
and
VI
, references to first-order logic implicitly means the variant
without equality
and with infinite conjunctions and disjunctions. We shall return to the variant with equality in Section
VII
. Note that logical equivalence of two
finite
structures in a fragment of logic with infinite conjunctions and disjunctions is the same as in the corresponding fragment of first-order logic with finite conjunctions and disjunctions.

We now consider FVM theorems for
\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{C}}
and
\equiv_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{C}}
, parametric in
\mathbb{C}
. To motivate our abstract machinery, we first consider a warm-up example, that is nevertheless sufficient to highlight the key ideas.
For a signature
\sigma
, and
\tau\subseteq\sigma
, the
\tau
-reduct
of a
\sigma
-structure
A
is the
\tau
-structure on the same universe which interprets all the relation symbols in
\tau
as in
A
.
We aim to show that equivalence in positive existential first-order logic with quantifier rank
{\leq}\,k
is preserved by taking
\tau
-reducts of
\sigma
-structures for any
\tau\subseteq\sigma
. We can view the
\tau
-reduct operation as a functor
(-)|_{\tau}\colon\mathcal{R}(\sigma)\to\mathcal{R}(\tau)
. Observe that taking reducts admits an FVM theorem for the Ehrenfeucht–Fraïssé comonad
{\mathbb{E}_{k}}
, viewed as a comonad on both
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
and
\mathcal{R}(\tau)
categories. Given the logical reading of
\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}
, it is an easy observation that
A\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}B
implies
A|_{\tau}\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}B|_{\tau}
. Indeed, if the positive existential sentences of quantifier depth
{\leq}\,k
in signature
\sigma
that are true in
A
are also true in
B
, then the same must hold for the positive existential sentences in the reduced signature
\tau
.

In this case, the FVM theorem can be proved using an ad-hoc argument about the operation involved. However, to identify a general strategy, we would like to prove this fact categorically. Given a morphism
f\colon{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(A)\to B
in
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
witnessing
A\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}B
, we wish to construct an
\overline{f}\colon{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(A|_{\tau})\to B|_{\tau}
witnessing
A|_{\tau}\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}B|_{\tau}
. To this end, observe that there is a morphism of
\tau
-structures

\kappa_{A}\colon{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(A|_{\tau})\to{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(A)|_{\tau}

which sends a word
[a_{1},\dots,a_{n}]
in
{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(A|_{\tau})
to the same word in
{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(A)|_{\tau}
. Therefore,
\overline{f}
can be computed as the composite

{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(A|_{\tau})\xrightarrow{\kappa_{A}}{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(A)|_{\tau}\xrightarrow{f|_{\tau}}B|_{\tau}.

(3)

It is immediate that, since the definition of
\kappa_{A}
was not specific to
A
, there is such a morphism for every structure. Consequently, we obtain the following trivial pair of FVM theorems:

\displaystyle A\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}B

\displaystyle\kern 5.0pt\mbox{implies}\kern 5.0ptA|_{\tau}\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}B|_{\tau}

\displaystyle A\equiv_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}B

\displaystyle\kern 5.0pt\mbox{implies}\kern 5.0ptA|_{\tau}\equiv_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}B|_{\tau}

As this exercise demonstrates, all that is required to prove an FVM theorem for the relations
\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}
or
\equiv_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}
, and a unary operation
H
, is to define for every structure
A
a morphism of
\sigma
-structures
\kappa_{A}\colon{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(H(A))\rightarrow H({\mathbb{E}_{k}}(A))
. It is not difficult to see that the same proof goes through when we parameterise over the comonads involved and allow operations of arbitrary arity. We obtain our first abstract FVM theorem.

Theorem III.2
.

Let
\mathbb{C}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{C}_{n}
and
\mathbb{D}
be comonads on categories
\mathcal{C}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{n}
, and
\mathcal{D}
respectively, and

H\colon\mathcal{C}_{1}\times\dots\times\mathcal{C}_{n}\to\mathcal{D}

a functor.
If for every
A_{1}\in\mathrm{obj}(\mathcal{C}_{1}),\,\dots,\,A_{n}\in\mathrm{obj}(\mathcal{C}_{n})
there exists a morphism

\mathbb{D}(H(A_{1},\dots,A_{n}))\xrightarrow{\!\!\!\overset{\kappa_{A_{1},\dots,A_{n}}}{\hskip 8.19447pt}\!\!\!}H(\mathbb{C}_{1}(A_{1}),\dots,\mathbb{C}_{n}(A_{n}))

(4)

in
\mathcal{D}
, then

A_{1}\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{C}_{1}}B_{1},\;\ldots,\;A_{n}\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{C}_{n}}B_{n}

implies

H(A_{1},\dots,A_{n})\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{D}}H(B_{1},\dots,B_{n})

The same result holds when replacing
\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{C}}
with
\equiv_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{C}}
.

Remark III.3

(Infinitary Operations)
.

For readability reasons, we state this as well as the other abstract FVM theorems, such as Theorems
IV.4
and
V.14
below, for operations of finite arities only. Nevertheless, all these statements hold verbatim for infinitary operations as well.

III-A

Examples

Example III.4
.

As our first simple application of Theorem
III.2
, we obtain an FVM theorem showing that
\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}
is preserved by taking the disjoint union
A_{1}\uplus A_{2}
of two
\sigma
-structures
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
.
The universe of
A_{1}\uplus A_{2}
can be encoded as pairs
(i,a)
where
i\in\{1,2\}
and
a\in A_{i}
.
For
r
-ary relation
R\in\sigma
, the interpretation
R^{A_{1}\uplus A_{2}}
is defined as

R^{A_{1}\uplus A_{2}}((i_{1},a_{1}),\dots,(i_{r},a_{r}))

if and only if there exists an
i
such that for all
1\leq j\leq r
,
i_{j}=i
and

R^{A_{i}}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r})
.
We define a morphism of
\sigma
-structures

\kappa_{A_{1},A_{2}}\colon{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(A_{1}\uplus A_{2})\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{k}}A_{1}\uplus{\mathbb{P}_{k}}A_{2}

which sends a word
s=[(p_{1},(i_{1},a_{1})),\dots,(p_{n},(i_{n},a_{n}))]
in
{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(A_{1}\uplus A_{2})
to the pair
(i_{n},\nu(s))
,
where the word
\nu(s)
is the restriction of
s
to the pairs
(p_{j},a_{j})
such that
i_{j}=i_{n}
.
Since the definition of
\kappa_{A_{1},A_{2}}
is not specific to the structures
A_{1},A_{2}
, by Theorem
III.2
, the following holds:

\displaystyle A_{1}\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}B_{1}\kern 5.0pt\mbox{and}\kern 5.0ptA_{2}\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}B_{2}

\displaystyle\mbox{implies}\quad A_{1}\uplus A_{2}\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}B_{1}\uplus B_{2}

Consequently, the same statement holds with
\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}
replaced by
\equiv_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}
.
The statement demonstrates that if both
A_{1}
and
B_{1}
, and
A_{2}
and
B_{2}
, satisfy the same sentences of the positive existential fragment of
k
-variable logic,
then the disjoint unions
A_{1}\uplus A_{2}
and
B_{1}\uplus B_{2}
also satisfy the same sentences of that fragment.
Similar
\kappa
morphisms can be defined to demonstrate that
\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}
,
\equiv_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}
and also
\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}
and
\equiv_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}
are preserved by taking disjoint unions of
arbitrary
sets of
\sigma
-structures.

Even in the case of relatively simple operations on structures, it may be the case that logical
equivalence is not preserved.

Counterexample III.5
.

For the modal fragment, a positive modal formula is one without negation or the
\Box
modality.
As shown in
[
11
]
, for pointed structures

A=(A,a)\kern 5.0pt\mbox{and}\kern 5.0ptB=(B,b)

in a modal signature,
A\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{M}_{k}}}B
if and only if for every positive modal formula
\varphi
of modal depth at most
k
,

A\models\varphi
implies
B\models\varphi
,
and therefore
A\equiv_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{M}_{k}}}B
if and only if the two structures agree on positive modal formulae with modal depth bounded by
k
.

Categorically, the disjoint union of
\sigma
-structures is their
coproduct
in
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
.
The formal definition of coproducts is unimportant for this example, but we note that
coproduct of two
pointed

\sigma
-structures
(A_{1},a_{1})+(A_{2},a_{2})
in
\mathcal{R}_{*}(\sigma)
is a quotient of their disjoint union, where the distinguished points
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
are identified.
Unlike with
{\mathbb{E}_{k}}
and
{\mathbb{P}_{k}}
, the relations
\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{M}_{k}}}
and
\equiv_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{M}_{k}}}
are
not
preserved by this operation on pointed
\sigma
-structures, essentially because the two components can interact.
This shows that although coproduct FVM theorems are relatively simple, it is by no means automatic that they will hold.

Example III.6
.

For every binary relation
R
in a modal signature
\sigma
, there is an operation
A_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R}}{{\vee}}A_{2}
which combines the two pointed structures by adding a new initial point
\star
with
R
-transitions to
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
. Operations such as this are commonly found when modelling concurrent systems with process calculi
[
31
,
32
]
. Intuitively this operation avoids the interactions that caused problems in Counterexample
III.5
.

There is a morphism of pointed
\sigma
-structures

\kappa_{A_{1},A_{2}}\colon\mathbb{M}_{k+1}(A_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R}}{{\vee}}A_{2})\rightarrow{\mathbb{M}_{k}}(A_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R}}{{\vee}}{\mathbb{M}_{k}}(A_{2})

All sequences in the domain are of the form

*\xrightarrow{R}(i,c_{0})\xrightarrow{R_{1}}\ldots\xrightarrow{R_{n}}(i,c_{n}),

with
i\in\{1,2\}
and each
(i,c_{j})\in A_{1}\uplus A_{2}
in the same component.
Then,
\kappa_{A_{1},A_{2}}
must preserve the distinguished element, and for sequences of length greater than one it sends
*\xrightarrow{R}(i,c_{0})\xrightarrow{R_{1}}\ldots\xrightarrow{R_{n}}(i,c_{n})
to
(i,c_{0}\xrightarrow{R_{1}}\ldots\xrightarrow{R_{n}}c_{n})
.
Applying Theorem
III.2
yields the FVM theorem:

\displaystyle A_{1}\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{M}_{k}}}B_{1}\kern 5.0pt\mbox{and}\kern 5.0ptA_{2}\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{M}_{k}}}B_{2}

\displaystyle\mbox{implies}\quad A_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R}}{{\vee}}A_{2}\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{M}_{k+1}}B_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R}}{{\vee}}B_{2}

Notice that the resource index actually
increases
from
k
to
k+1
because of the shape of
\kappa_{A_{1},A_{2}}
.

Example III.7
.

A nice feature of the generality of Theorem
III.2
is that for different comonads
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{D}
over the same category
\mathcal{C}
, a natural transformation between the functors
\mathbb{C}\Rightarrow\mathbb{D}
or, more generally, a collection of morphisms

\{\kappa_{A}\colon\mathbb{D}A\rightarrow\mathbb{C}A\mid A\in\mathrm{obj}(\mathcal{C})\}

can be seen as instances of (
4
), with
H
equal to the identity functor
{I\mkern-1.0mud}\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
.
This allows us to provide semantic translations for the logics captured by game comonads, a question that had not previously been addressed.
For example, if we consider
\mathbb{P}_{2}
as a comonad over
\mathcal{R}_{*}(\sigma)
where
\mathbb{P}_{2}(A,a_{0})
has distinguished point
[(0,a_{0})]
, then for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
and object
(A,a_{0})
we define a morphism of pointed
\sigma
-structures

\kappa_{(A,a_{0})}\colon{\mathbb{M}_{k}}(A,a_{0})\rightarrow\mathbb{P}_{2}(A,a_{0})

which sends the sequence of transitions

a_{0}\xrightarrow{R_{1}}\ldots\xrightarrow{R_{n-1}}a_{n-1}\xrightarrow{R_{n}}a_{n}

in
{\mathbb{M}_{k}}(A,a_{0})

to the word in
\mathbb{P}_{2}(A,a_{0})
where elements are labeled by the parity of their position

[(0,a_{0}),(1,a_{1}),\dots,(n\bmod 2,a_{n})].

Applying Theorem
III.2
yields the well-known fact that structures which satisfy the same sentences of the positive existential fragment of two-variable logic must also satisfy the same positive modal formulas for any modal depth
k

\displaystyle(A,a_{0})\equiv_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{P}_{2}}(B,b_{0})

\displaystyle\mbox{implies}\quad(\forall k\in\mathbb{N}.\ \ (A,a_{0})\equiv_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{M}_{k}}}(B,b_{0})).

Similarly, there exist morphisms

\kappa_{A}\colon{\mathbb{E}_{k}}A\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{k}}A
for every
A
in
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
where
[a_{1},\dots,a_{n}]
with
n\leq k
is sent to
[(0,a_{1}),\dots(n-1,a_{n})]
demonstrating that
\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}
refines
\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}
.

IV
FVM theorems for counting logic

We now consider another relationship induced by a game comonad. To do so, we first introduce one of the two standard categories associated with any comonad.

The
Kleisli category

[
33
]

\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{C})
for a comonad
\mathbb{C}
on
\mathcal{C}
, has the same objects as
\mathcal{C}
. The morphisms of type
A\rightarrow B
in
\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{C})
are the morphisms of type
\mathbb{C}(A)\to B
in
\mathcal{C}
. To avoid ambiguity, we shall write
f:A\to_{\mathbb{C}}B
if we intend
f
to be understood as a Kleisli morphism. The identity morphism at
A
is the counit component
\varepsilon_{A}:\mathbb{C}(A)\rightarrow A
.
As composition is different to that in the base category, we use the distinct notation
g\mathbin{\bullet}f
for the composite of morphisms

f\colon A\to_{\mathbb{C}}B
and
g\colon B\to_{\mathbb{C}}C
,
which is defined as
g\circ f^{*}
, where we recall
f^{*}:\mathbb{C}(A)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}(B)
is the coextension of
f\colon\mathbb{C}(A)\to B
. The axioms for a comonad in Kleisli form ensure that this is a well-defined category. Notice that the morphisms in the Kleisli category are exactly those that were important for positive existential fragments in Section
III
.

As with any category, we can consider the isomorphisms in
\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{C})
. We shall write
A\equiv_{\#\mathbb{C}}B
if structures
A
and
B
are isomorphic in the Kleisli category. For finite structures, the relations
A\equiv_{\#{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}B
and
A\equiv_{\#{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}B
correspond to equivalence in counting logic
[
10
,
11
]
. The ideas of Theorem
III.2
can be extended to establish an FVM theorem for counting fragments and the
\equiv_{\#\mathbb{C}}
relation. All that is required is to impose extra conditions on the collection of morphisms (
4
). Furthermore, these extra conditions turn out to be those of the well-known Kleisli laws related to lifting functors to Kleisli categories
[
25
]
.

To motivate our comonadic method, we return to the minimal reduct example from Section
III
. We can show that
A\equiv_{\#{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}B
implies
A|_{\tau}\equiv_{\#{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}B|_{\tau}
,
via a similar ad-hoc argument to before.
In our setting, we wish to establish that, for mutually inverse morphisms

f\colon A\to_{{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}B
and
g\colon B\to_{{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}A
in
\mathsf{Kl}({\mathbb{E}_{k}})
,
the composites
f|_{\tau}\circ\kappa_{A}
and
g|_{\tau}\circ\kappa_{B}
, constructed as in (
3
), are also mutually inverse in the Kleisli category.
In the following we analyse the abstract setting in which the relation
\equiv_{\#\mathbb{C}}
is defined and derive a general result, similar to Theorem
III.2
.

IV-A

Kleisli laws

Akin to our motivating example, we assume a unary operation
H:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
,
comonads
(\mathbb{C},\varepsilon^{\mathbb{C}},(-)^{*})
on
\mathcal{C}
and
(\mathbb{D},\varepsilon^{\mathbb{D}},(-)^{*})
on
\mathcal{D}
, and also a collection of morphisms

\{\kappa_{A}\colon\mathbb{D}H(A)\to H\mathbb{C}(A)\mid A\in\mathrm{obj}(\mathcal{C})\},

as in Theorem
III.2
. We wish to find conditions on this collection of morphisms ensuring that
A\equiv_{\#\mathbb{C}}B
implies
H(A)\equiv_{\#\mathbb{D}}H(B)
. Rephrasing, we require axioms such that if
f\colon A\to_{\mathbb{C}}B
and
g\colon B\to_{\mathbb{C}}A
are mutually inverse, then the composites

H(f)\circ\kappa_{A}\colon H(A)\to_{\mathbb{D}}H(B)

and

H(g)\circ\kappa_{B}\colon H(B)\to_{\mathbb{D}}H(A)

are also mutually inverse. To do so, we observe that if both

H(\varepsilon_{A})\circ\kappa_{A}=\varepsilon_{H(A)}

(5)

and

H(f^{*})\circ\kappa_{A}=\kappa_{B}\circ(H(f)\circ\kappa_{A})^{*}

(6)

then, from
g\mathbin{\bullet}f=\mathrm{id}
in
\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{D})
(i.e.
g\circ f^{*}=\varepsilon_{A}
), we obtain that

\displaystyle(H(g)\circ\kappa_{B})\mathbin{\bullet}(H(f)\circ\kappa_{A})

\displaystyle=H(g)\circ\kappa_{B}\circ(H(f)\circ\kappa_{A})^{*}

definition

\displaystyle=H(g)\circ H(f^{*})\circ\kappa_{A}

(
6
)

\displaystyle=H(g\circ f^{*})\circ\kappa_{A}

functoriality

\displaystyle=H(g\mathbin{\bullet}f)\circ\kappa_{A}

definition

\displaystyle=H(\varepsilon_{A})\circ\kappa_{A}

assumption

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{H(A)}:\mathbb{D}(H(A))\to H(A)

(
5
)

and, similarly,

f\mathbin{\bullet}g=\mathrm{id}
implies that

(H(f)\circ\kappa_{A})\mathbin{\bullet}(H(g)\circ\kappa_{B})

equals to identity in
\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{D})
.
In fact, equations (
5
) and (
6
) are equivalent to requiring that
\kappa
is a
Kleisli law

[
25
]
.

Lemma IV.1
.

A collection of morphisms
\{\kappa_{A}\}
satisfies equations (
5
) and (
6
) for every
f\colon\mathbb{C}(A)\to B
iff
\kappa
is a natural transformation
\mathbb{D}\circ H\Rightarrow H\circ\mathbb{C}
such that the following commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}\circ H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\circ\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\varepsilon}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}\circ H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}^{2}\circ H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}\circ H\circ\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\circ\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\circ\mathbb{C}^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{\mathbb{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\delta}

Remark IV.2
.

Kleisli laws correspond precisely to liftings of the operation
H\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
to operations
\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{C})\to\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{D})
commuting with the cofree functors
\mathcal{C}\to\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{C})
and
\mathcal{D}\to\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{D})
. See for example
[
34
]
.
Furthermore, a Kleisli law
\kappa:\mathbb{C}\Rightarrow\mathbb{D}
with respect to the identity functor is the same thing as a
comonad morphism
.

To generalise to operations of arbitrary arities, we first introduce some notation.
For a set
I
, and family of categories
\{\mathcal{C}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
, we shall write

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle A_{i}\hfil

for a family of objects, with each
A_{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}
, and similarly

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle f_{i}\hfil

for families of morphisms. These form the
product category

\prod_{i\in I}\mathcal{C}_{i}
, with composition and identities defined componentwise. Given a family of comonads
\mathbb{C}_{i}:\mathcal{C}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{i}
, they induce a comonad
\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}_{i}
on the product category, again componentwise.

Using this notation, we define the
n
-ary version of the axioms from Lemma
IV.1
.
Kleisli laws
for an
n
-ary operation
H
are the natural transformations
\kappa\colon\mathbb{D}\circ H\Rightarrow H\circ\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}_{i}
such that the following diagrams commute:

(K1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}\circ H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\circ\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\prod_{i}\varepsilon)}

(K2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}\circ H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}^{2}\circ H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}\circ H\circ\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\circ\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\circ\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}^{2}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\prod_{i}\delta)}

Remark IV.3
.

To reduce clutter, in the axioms above and in the rest of this text we often omit the subscripts for the components of natural transformations, if they can be easily inferred from the context.

The previous argument generalises smoothly to operations of any arity. We obtain our FVM theorem for counting fragments.

Theorem IV.4
.

Let
\mathbb{C}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{C}_{n}
and
\mathbb{D}
be comonads on categories
\mathcal{C}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{n}
and
\mathcal{D}
, respectively, and

H\colon\prod_{i}\mathcal{C}_{i}\to\mathcal{D}

a functor.
If there exists a Kleisli law of type

\mathbb{D}\circ H\Rightarrow H\circ\prod\nolimits_{i}\mathbb{C}_{i}

then

A_{1}\equiv_{\#\mathbb{C}_{1}}B_{1}\;\ldots,\;A_{n}\equiv_{\#\mathbb{C}_{n}}B_{n}

implies

H(A_{1},\dots,A_{n})\;\equiv_{\#\mathbb{D}}\;H(B_{1},\dots,B_{n}).

IV-B

Examples

Example IV.5
.

The collection of
\kappa
morphisms described in each of
Examples
III.4
,
III.6
and
III.7
of Section
III-A

are natural and satisfy axioms
(K1)
and
(K2)
.
Therefore, by applying Theorem
IV.4
to the Kleisli laws in
Examples
III.4
and
III.6

we have that
\equiv_{\#{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}
and
\equiv_{\#{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}
are preserved by taking coproducts of structures and the merge operation maps
\equiv_{\#{\mathbb{M}_{k}}}
to
\equiv_{\#\mathbb{M}_{k+1}}
.
Recalling Remark
IV.2
, by applying Theorem
IV.4
to the comonad morphism in Example
III.7
we have that for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\equiv_{\#\mathbb{P}_{2}}
refines
\equiv_{\#{\mathbb{M}_{k}}}
, and
\equiv_{\#{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}
refines
\equiv_{\#{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}
.

Example IV.6

(Cospectrality)
.

Two graphs
G
and
H
are
cospectral
if the adjacency matrices of
G
and
H
have the same multiset of eigenvalues.
We shall use Theorem
IV.4
to strengthen a result in
[
28
]
showing that equivalence in 3-variable counting logic
with equality
implies cospectrality.

Since cospectrality is a notion on graphs, we move to the full subcategory of loopless undirected graphs in signature
\sigma=\{E\}
where
E
is a binary edge relation.
The comonad
{\mathbb{P}_{k}}
restricts to this category and we also consider a comonad
\mathtt{Cos}
defined in
[
35
, Section 3]
which characterizes cospectrality,
in that

G\equiv_{\#\mathtt{Cos}}H
is equivalent to
G
and
H
being cospectral.
The universe of
\mathtt{Cos}(G)
can be encoded as pairs
(c,v_{i})
where
c
is a closed walk
v_{0}\xrightarrow{E}v_{1}\xrightarrow{E}\ldots\xrightarrow{E}v_{n}\xrightarrow{E}v_{0}
that passes through
v_{i}
.
Two pairs
(c,v_{i}),(c^{\prime},v_{j})
are adjacent in
\mathtt{Cos}(G)
if
c=c^{\prime}
and
v_{i}
is adjacent to
v_{j}
in the closed walk
c
.
The counit
\varepsilon_{G}
maps
(c,v_{i})
to
v_{i}
.
For
h\colon\mathtt{Cos}(G)\rightarrow H
, the coextension
h^{*}
maps the pair
(c,v_{i})
with
c
being the closed walk
v_{0}\xrightarrow{E}v_{1}\xrightarrow{E}\ldots\xrightarrow{E}v_{n}\xrightarrow{E}v_{0}
to
(d,h(c,v_{i}))
where
d
is the closed walk
h(c,v_{0})\xrightarrow{E}h(c,v_{1})\ldots\xrightarrow{E}h(c,v_{n})\xrightarrow{E}h(c,v_{0})
.

We define a comonad morphism
\kappa\colon\mathtt{Cos}(G)\to\mathbb{P}_{3}(G)
where
(c,v_{i})
is mapped to the word

[(2,v_{0}),(1,v_{1}),(0,v_{2}),(1,v_{3}),\dots,(i\bmod 2,v_{i})].

Recalling Remark
IV.2
, we apply Theorem
IV.4
, deducing that
\equiv_{\#\mathbb{P}_{3}}
implies
\equiv_{\#\mathtt{Cos}}
.
Since
\equiv_{\#\mathbb{P}_{3}}
captures equivalence in 3-variable counting logic
without equality
and
\equiv_{\#\mathtt{Cos}}
captures cospectrality, we have avoided the need for equality in the logic.
This fact is also a consequence of
[
19
, Theorem 32]
and the original theorem of Dawar, Severini, and Zapata
[
28
]
.
However, the same reasoning allows us to define a comonad morphism
\mathtt{Cos}\Rightarrow\mathbb{PR}_{3}
where
\mathbb{PR}_{3}
is the pebble-relation comonad from
[
17
]
, capturing the restricted conjunction fragment of
3
-variable counting logic.
The universe of
\mathbb{PR}_{3}(G)
consists of pairs
(s,i)
with
s\in\mathbb{P}_{3}(G)
is a sequence of length
n
and
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
is an index into the sequence
s
.
This proves a genuine strengthening of the previous result.

V
FVM theorems for the full logic

In Sections
III
and
IV
we described FVM theorems for the equivalence relations
\equiv_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{C}}
and
\equiv_{\#\mathbb{C}}
, typically expressing logical equivalence for the positive existential and counting logic variants. To do the same for the full logic requires us to move from the Kleisli category to the richer setting of the
Eilenberg–Moore category of coalgebras
.

For a comonad
\mathbb{C}
on
\mathcal{C}
, a pair
(A,\alpha)
where
\alpha\colon A\to\mathbb{C}(A)
is a morphism in
\mathcal{C}
is a
\mathbb{C}
-coalgebra
or simply just
coalgebra
if the following two diagrams commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{A}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ \mathbb{C}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(\mathbb{C}(A))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(\alpha)}

(7)

A morphism of coalgebras

f\colon(A,\alpha)\to(B,\beta)
is a morphism
f\colon A\to B
such that
\beta\circ f=\mathbb{C}(f)\circ\alpha
. We write
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
for the Eilenberg–Moore category of coalgebras of
\mathbb{C}
.

Remark V.1
.

A coalgebra
(A,\alpha)
of any of the game comonads defined in Section
II-B
is endowed with a preorder
\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}
, where
x\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}y
whenever the word
\alpha(x)
is a prefix of
\alpha(y)
.
A
forest order
is a poset in which the set of predecessors of any element in a finite chain. It follows from the axioms in (
7
) that
\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}
is a forest order on the universe.

For example, the category
\mathsf{EM}({\mathbb{E}_{k}})
is equivalent to the category of
\sigma
-structures endowed with a compatible forest order of depth
{\leq}\,k
. Similar characterisations of the categories
\mathsf{EM}({\mathbb{P}_{k}})
and
\mathsf{EM}({\mathbb{M}_{k}})
can be made as well, cf.
[
11
, Section 9]
.

We are now almost ready to define the relation
\equiv_{\mathbb{C}}
.
Given
A,B\in\mathcal{C}
, define
A\equiv_{\mathbb{C}}B

if there exists a span of
open pathwise-embeddings

F^{\mathbb{C}}(A)\leftarrow X\rightarrow F^{\mathbb{C}}(B)

where
F^{\mathbb{C}}(A)
is the
cofree coalgebra
on
A
, concretely this is the pair
(\mathbb{C}(A),\delta_{A})
.
We postpone the definition of open pathwise-embeddings to Section
V-B
.

In terms of our example comonads,
A\equiv_{{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}B
and
A\equiv_{{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}B
correspond to agreement on first-order sentences of
k
-bounded quantifier depth and variable count respectively
[
11
]
. Similarly,
(A,a)\equiv_{{\mathbb{M}_{k}}}(B,b)
characterises agreement on modal formulae of modal depth at most
k
.

Returning again to the example of reducts, showing the trivial fact that
A\equiv_{{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}B
implies
A|_{\tau}\equiv_{{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}B|_{\tau}
in our setting suggests the strategy:

1.

Lift an
n
-ary operation
H
to the level of coalgebras, in a manner that commutes with the construction
F^{\mathbb{C}}(-)
of cofree coalgebras.

2.

Check that open pathwise-embeddings are preserved by the lifted operation.

Tackling step
1
is the topic of Section
V-A
. Step
2
is the subject of the subsequent Section
V-B
.

V-A

Lifting operations to coalgebras

Here we focus on the task of lifting a given operation to the level of coalgebras.
By a
lifting
of a functor
H:\prod_{i}\mathcal{C}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
we mean a functor

\widehat{H}:\prod_{i}\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C}_{i})\rightarrow\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})
such that the following diagram commutes
up to isomorphism:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C}_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}\mathcal{C}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}F^{\mathbb{C}_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\mathbb{D}}}

To this end, we generalise a standard technique from monad theory, used for lifting adjunctions to the Eilenberg–Moore categories
[
36
]
or to lifting monoidal structure to the algebras of commutative monad
[
37
]
(see also
[
34
,
38
,
27
]
).
For such a lifting to exist, given a Kleisli law, it is sufficient that the codomain category of coalgebras has sufficient equalisers.
In our case, it is enough to check that the comonad on the target category preserves embeddings and the rest is ensured by Linton’s theorem
[
39
]
.

In the following we say that a homomorphism of (pointed)
\sigma
-structures
f\colon A\to B
is an
embedding
, written
f\colon A\rightarrowtail B
, if it is injective and reflects relations.
Further, we say that a comonad
\mathbb{C}

preserves embeddings
if
\mathbb{C}(f)
is an embedding whenever
f
is. In similar fashion, an
n
-ary operation
H
preserves embeddings if
H(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})
is an embedding whenever
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}
are embeddings in their respective categories.
All the comonads that we have introduced thus far preserve embeddings.

Remark V.2
.

For concreteness, in the following
\mathcal{C}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{n},\mathcal{D}
are categories of
\sigma
-structures or pointed
\sigma
-structures.
Nevertheless, a categorically minded reader will readily see that our statements hold more generally, typically for categories which have coproducts, a
proper
2
2

2

As
proper
we identify any factorisation system
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
with
\mathcal{E}\subseteq\text{epis}
and
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\text{monos}
.
factorisation system
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
and are
\mathcal{M}
-well-powered.

The following theorem allows us to lift a functor
H\colon\prod_{i}\mathcal{C}_{i}\to\mathcal{D}
if the comonad on the target category preserves embeddings.

Theorem V.3
.

If
\kappa
is a Kleisli law of type
\mathbb{D}\circ H\Rightarrow H\circ\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}_{i}
and
\mathbb{D}
preserves embeddings then the lifting of
H
to
\widehat{H}
exists.

In what follows, we do not need to understand how exactly
\widehat{H}
is computed. However, we often need to analyse morphisms of the form
(A,\alpha)\to\widehat{H}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle(B_{i},\beta_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle(B_{i},\beta_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle(B_{i},\beta_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern , for some coalgebras

. To aid with this we restate a direct generalisation of the universal property of bimorphisms, known from linear algebra and also from the setting of commutative monads

]

V-B

Open pathwise-embeddings and their preservation

In this section we define open pathwise-embeddings and give sufficient conditions for the lifted functors of Section
V-A
to preserve them. In fact, our condition is a combination of two other categorical properties: parametric adjoints and relative adjoints, which we discuss in Section
V-C
.

The coalgebras of game comonads are structures carrying a forest order compatible with their underlying
\sigma
-structures. We fix a collection of, typically linearly ordered, coalgebras
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
which we call
paths
. In concrete applications, there is typically a natural choice of paths, and we will leave this parameter implicit in the theorems that follow.
We call a morphism of coalgebras
(A,\alpha)\to(B,\beta)
an
embedding
if the underlying morphism
A\to B
is an embedding of
\sigma
-structures, and a
path embedding
if furthermore
(A,\alpha)
is a path.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

A morphism
f\colon X\to Y
in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
is a
pathwise-embedding
if
f\circ e
is an embedding for every path embedding
e\colon P\rightarrowtail X
in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
. Further,
f
is
open
if for every
commutative square as shown on the right,
where
e,m
are path embeddings,
there exists a morphism
d\colon Q\to X
such that
e=d\circ g
and
m=f\circ d
.

Remark V.5
.

Recall from Remark
V.1
that coalgebras
(A,\alpha)
, for our example comonads,
{\mathbb{E}_{k}},{\mathbb{P}_{k}},
or
{\mathbb{M}_{k}}
, come equipped with a forest order
\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}
on the universe. In the following, we always assume that path coalgebras of these comonads are the coalgebras
(A,\alpha)
which are finite linear orders in
\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}
.

We now come back to our original goal. In order for an
n
-ary operation
H\colon\prod_{i}\mathcal{C}_{i}\to\mathcal{D}
to admit an FVM theorem, we wish to show that its lifting

\widehat{H}\colon\prod\nolimits_{i}\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C}_{i})\to\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})

described in Theorem
V.3
, sends tuples of open pathwise embeddings to pathwise embeddings.

In the remainder of this section we assume the following two conditions and show that they suffice:

(S1)

\widehat{H}\colon\prod_{i}\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C}_{i})\to\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})
preserves embeddings.

(S2)

0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle X_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle X_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle X_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}})
,
for some path embeddings
g_{i}\colon Q_{i}\rightarrowtail X_{i}
, there exist necessarily unique morphisms
h_{i}\colon P_{i}\to Q_{i}
such that
e_{i}=g_{i}\circ h_{i}
, for
i=1,\dots,n
.

We are going to need the following basic properties of embeddings in categories of coalgebras, derived from the properties of embeddings in
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
and
\mathcal{R}_{*}(\sigma)
.

Lemma V.6
.

Let
\mathbb{C}
be a comonad over a category of (pointed) relational structures. Then, the following hold for embeddings in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
:

1.

Every embedding
e
is a monomorphism, that is, if
e\circ f=e\circ g
then
f=g
.

2.

Embeddings are closed under composition.

3.

If
g\circ f
is an embedding then so is
f
.

Remark V.7
.

Categorically speaking, the above statement only restates properties of factorisation systems lifted to the category of coalgebras. Namely, (pointed) relational structures come equipped with a proper factorisation system
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
where
\mathcal{E}
are surjective homomorphisms and
\mathcal{M}
are embeddings. Then, it is a standard fact that, for
\mathbb{C}
which preserves embeddings,
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
admits a proper factorisation system
(\overline{\mathcal{E}},\overline{\mathcal{M}})
where a morphism of coalgebras
h
is in
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
(resp.
\overline{\mathcal{M}}
) if the underlying homomorphisms of
h
is in
\mathcal{E}
(resp.
\mathcal{M}
). Furthermore, surjective homomorphisms and embeddings of (pointed) relational structures are precisely epimorphisms and regular monomorphism, respectively. In fact, regular monos are the same as strong, extremal, or effective monos in this category.

These basic properties of embeddings listed in Lemma
V.6
allow us to show the first half of our desired result, preservation of pathwise-embeddings.

Lemma V.8
.

If
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}
are pathwise-embeddings in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C}_{1})
, …,
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C}_{n})
, respectively, then so is
\widehat{H}(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})
.

Proof.

Assume
e\colon(P,\pi)\rightarrowtail\widehat{H}\bigl(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle(A_{i},\alpha_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle(A_{i},\alpha_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle(A_{i},\alpha_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle(A_{i},\alpha_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}}\bigr)
is a path embedding where, for
i=1,\dots,n
, the coalgebra
(A_{i},\alpha_{i})
is the domain of
f_{i}
. By
(S2)
,
e
decomposes as
e_{0}\colon(P,\pi)\rightarrowtail\widehat{H}\bigl(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle(P_{i},\pi_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle(P_{i},\pi_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle(P_{i},\pi_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern followed by

\widehat{H}\bigl(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle e_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle(A_{i},\alpha_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle(A_{i},\alpha_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle(A_{i},\alpha_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle(A_{i},\alpha_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}}\bigr).

Observe that, by Lemma
V.6
.3,
e_{0}
is an embedding because
e
is. Further, since
f_{i}
is a pathwise-embedding, for
i=1,\dots,n
, the morphism
f_{i}\circ e_{i}
is an embedding. Therefore, by
(S1)
,
\widehat{H}\bigl(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle f_{i}\circ e_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle f_{i}\circ e_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle f_{i}\circ e_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle f_{i}\circ e_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}\bigr)
is also an embedding. We obtain that the composite
\widehat{H}\bigl(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle f_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle f_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle f_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle f_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}\bigr)\circ e=\widehat{H}\bigl(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle f_{i}\circ e_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle f_{i}\circ e_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle f_{i}\circ e_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle f_{i}\circ e_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}\bigr)\circ e_{0}
is an embedding because embeddings are closed under composition, cf. Lemma
V.6
.2.
∎

For the preservation of openness, we need the following technical lemma.

Lemma V.9
.

Let
f_{i}\colon(A_{i},\alpha_{i})\to(B_{i},\beta_{i})
in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C}_{i})
be pathwise-embeddings, for

, and let

and

be path embeddings making the diagram on the left below commute.

be the embedding such that

\widehat{H}\left(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern is the minimal decomposition of

by

. Since

is another decomposition of

, there exists morphisms

such that

, for

.

Next, we observe that
e_{0}=\widehat{H}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern , given by
\widehat{H}\bigl(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle e_{i}\circ l_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle e_{i}\circ l_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle e_{i}\circ l_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle e_{i}\circ l_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}\bigr)\circ g_{0}
. By minimality

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle e_{i}\hfil

, there exists

f^{\prime}_{i}\colon(P_{i},\pi_{i})\to(Q_{i},\rho_{i}),

for
i=1,\dots,n
, such that
e_{i}=e_{i}\circ l_{i}\circ f^{\prime}_{i}
. Therefore, for
i=1,\dots,n
,

f_{i}\circ e_{i}=f_{i}\circ e_{i}\circ l_{i}\circ f^{\prime}_{i}=g_{i}\circ f^{\prime}_{i}.\qed

We are now ready to proof the main theorem of this section.

Theorem V.10
.

If
\widehat{H}
satisfies
(S1)
and
(S2)
and
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}
are open pathwise-embeddings, then so is
\widehat{H}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle f_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle f_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern .

, open pathwise-embeddings

in

, it is enough to check that

. Assume that the outer square of path embeddings in the diagram below commutes, with the left-most and right-most morphisms being their minimal decompositions by
