The path embedding
\widehat{H}\bigl(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle f_{i}\circ e_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle f_{i}\circ e_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle f_{i}\circ e_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle f_{i}\circ e_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}\bigr)\circ e_{0}
has a minimal decomposition going via

\widehat{H}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle e^{\prime}_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle e^{\prime}_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle e^{\prime}_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle e^{\prime}_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}})
as shown above.
Then, by minimality of this decomposition and by Lemma
V.9
, for
i=1,\dots,n
, there exist
f^{\prime}_{i}\colon(P_{i},\pi_{i})\to(P^{\prime}_{i},\pi^{\prime}_{i})
and
h_{i}\colon(P^{\prime}_{i},\pi^{\prime}_{i})\to(Q_{i},\rho_{i})
such that
f_{i}\circ e_{i}=e^{\prime}_{i}\circ f^{\prime}_{i}
and
e^{\prime}_{i}=g_{i}\circ h_{i}
. Since
f_{i}
is open and
f_{i}\circ e_{i}=g_{i}\circ h_{i}\circ f^{\prime}_{i}
, there is a morphism
d_{i}\colon\rho_{i}\to\alpha_{i}
such that
d_{i}\circ h_{i}\circ f^{\prime}_{i}=e_{i}
and
g_{i}=d_{i}\circ f_{i}
. Finally, because the outer rectangle and the bottom rectangle commute and
\widehat{H}\left(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle g_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle g_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle g_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle g_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}\right)
is a monomorphism (cf. Lemma
V.6
.1), the top rectangle commute as well. Consequently,
\widehat{H}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle d_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle d_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle d_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle d_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}})\circ g_{0}\colon(Q,\rho)\to\widehat{H}\bigl(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle(A_{i},\alpha_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle(A_{i},\alpha_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle(A_{i},\alpha_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle(A_{i},\alpha_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}}\bigr)
is the required diagonal filler of the outer square.
∎

V-C

Parametric relative right adjoints

In this section we show how the axioms
(S1)
and
(S2)
relate to mathematical concepts that have previously appeared in the literature, specifically parametric relative adjoints.
Parametric adjoints, also sometimes called local adjoints, originate in the work of Street
[
40
]
.
We use their equivalent definition due to Weber
[
41
,
42
,
43
]
.
Relative adjoints are a common tool in the semantics of programming languages, see for example
[
44
]
, and are connected to relative comonads which can be used to encapsulate the translations discussed in Section
VII

[
11
]
. We will be interested in a combination of these two notions.

We first review the required terminology. By
\mathcal{T}\mathbin{\downarrow}A
we denote the

slice category

consisting of pairs
(X,f)
, where
f\colon X\to A
is a morphism in
\mathcal{T}
. A morphism
(X,f)\to(Y,g)
between objects in
\mathcal{T}\mathbin{\downarrow}A
is a morphism
h\colon X\to Y
such that
f=g\circ h
. A functor
{F\colon\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{S}}
is said to be a
parametric right adjoint
if, for each object
A
of
\mathcal{C}
, the functor

F_{A}:\mathcal{T}\mathbin{\downarrow}A\to\mathcal{S}\mathbin{\downarrow}F(A)

sending
f\colon X\to A
to
F(f)\colon F(X)\to F(A)
, is a right adjoint.
Finally, a functor
R\colon\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{U}
is a
relative right adjoint
between a category
\mathcal{T}
and a functor
I\colon\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{U}
if there is a functor
L\colon\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{T}
and a natural bijection between sets of morphisms

L(S)\to T
in
\mathcal{T}

I(S)\to R(T)
in
\mathcal{U}

These definitions motivate our definition of parametric relative adjoints for categories of coalgebras with a selected class of path objects and a class of embeddings.

For simplicity we consider unary
\widehat{H}:\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})\to\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})
.
We write
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{D}}
for the class of paths in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
and
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})
respectively.

For
D\in\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})
, let
I_{D}
be the inclusion functor

I_{D}\colon\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{D}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}D\to\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}D

where
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}D
and
{\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{D}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}D}
denote the full subcategories of the comma category
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})\mathbin{\downarrow}D
consisting of pairs
(X,e)
where
e
is an embedding or a path embedding, respectively.

\widehat{H}
is a
parametric relative right adjoint
if, for every
C\in\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
, the functor

\widehat{H}_{C}\colon\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}C\kern 5.0pt\longrightarrow\kern 5.0pt\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}\widehat{H}(C),

which sends a path embedding
e
, with
e\colon X\rightarrowtail C
, to the embedding
\widehat{H}(e)
of type
\widehat{H}(X)\rightarrowtail\widehat{H}(C)
, is a relative right adjoint between
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}C
and
I_{\widehat{H}(C)}
. Following the above terminology, the last condition assumes the existence of a functor
L\colon\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{D}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}\widehat{H}(C)\to\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}C

such that there is a natural bijection between morphisms:

L(e)\to f
in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}C

I(e)\to\widehat{H}_{C}(f)
in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}\widehat{H}(C)

Proposition V.11
.

Assuming
(S1)
,
\widehat{H}
satisfies
(S2)
if and only if
\widehat{H}
is a parametric relative right adjoint.

V-D

Simplifying the axioms

In this section we simplify axioms
(S1)
and
(S2)
so that we can establish their validity easily, without having to analyse the lifted functor
\widehat{H}
.
As before, we assume that
H
lifts to
\widehat{H}
as in Theorem
V.3
.

To begin with, we observe that
(S1)
is implied by a similar property of
H
.

Proposition V.12
.

If
H
preserves embeddings then so does
\widehat{H}
.

By Proposition
V.4
we observe that
(S2)
is implied by a simple condition which can be stated entirely in the base category of (pointed) relational structures.

Proposition V.13
.

The axiom
(S2)
is implied by the following axiom:

(S2’)

For any path

in

0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle P_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}})\to H(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}),

for some path embeddings
e_{i}\colon(P_{i},\pi_{i})\rightarrowtail(A_{i},\alpha_{i})
.

In fact,
(S2)
is equivalent to the version of
(S2’)
where we only care about morphisms
f\colon P\to H(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}})
which arise from embeddings via the correspondence in Proposition
V.4
.

As a corollary, we obtain an FVM theorem for the full logic.

Theorem V.14
.

Let
\mathbb{C}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{C}_{n}
and
\mathbb{D}
be comonads on categories
\mathcal{C}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{n}
and
\mathcal{D}
, respectively, and

H\colon\prod\nolimits_{i}\mathcal{C}_{i}\to\mathcal{D}
a functor which preserves embeddings.
If there exists a Kleisli law of type

\mathbb{D}\circ H\Rightarrow H\circ\prod\nolimits_{i}\mathbb{C}_{i}

satisfying
(S2’)
, then

A_{1}\equiv_{\mathbb{C}_{1}}B_{1},\;\ldots,\;A_{n}\equiv_{\mathbb{C}_{n}}B_{n}

implies

H(A_{1},\dots,A_{n})\;\equiv_{\mathbb{D}}\;H(B_{1},\dots,B_{n}).

Example V.15
.

We can apply Theorem
V.14
to show that
\equiv_{{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}
is preserved by taking disjoint unions of structures.
As the functor
\uplus
preserves embeddings, we only have to check
(S2’)
for the Kleisli law
\kappa_{A_{1},A_{2}}\colon{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(A_{1}\uplus A_{2})\to{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(A_{1})\uplus{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(A_{2})
defined in Example
III.4
.
For a path
(P,\pi)
in
\mathsf{EM}({\mathbb{P}_{k}})
, consider a morphism
f\colon P\rightarrow A_{1}\uplus A_{2}
, where
(A_{1},\alpha_{1})
and
(A_{2},\alpha_{2})
are
{\mathbb{P}_{k}}
-coalgebras, and
f
makes the following diagram of
(S2’)
commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}\uplus A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(\alpha_{1}\uplus\alpha_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(A_{1})\uplus{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(A_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}\uplus\alpha_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}

Recall from Remark
V.1
that the coalgebra map
\pi
defines a forest order
\sqsubseteq_{\pi}
on the universe of
P
, and similarly for coalgebra maps
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
. Furthermore, since
(P,\pi)
is a path,
(P,\sqsubseteq_{\pi})
is a finite linear order
x_{1}\sqsubseteq_{\pi}\dots\sqsubseteq_{\pi}x_{n}
.

We may assume without loss of generality that
f(x_{n})
is in
A_{1}
. Then,
(\alpha_{1}\uplus\alpha_{2})(f(x_{i}))
gives a word in
{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(\alpha_{1})
, which forms a finite linear order in the
\sqsubseteq_{\alpha_{i}}
order. By commutativity of the above diagram, this must be the same word as the down-to-right composition
\kappa({\mathbb{P}_{k}}(f)(\pi(x_{n})))
. But, by definition, the latter is just the reduction of the word on elements
f(x_{1}),\dots,f(x_{n})
to the positions where
f(x_{i})\in A_{1}
.

The same reasoning applies for the largest
j
such that
f(x_{j})
is in
A_{2}
.
Each of the two words obtained this way yields a path embedding
e_{i}\colon(P_{i},\pi_{i})\rightarrowtail(A_{i},\alpha_{i})
as the embedding of the induced substructure of
A_{i}
on given word letters.
Define a morphism
e_{0}\colon P\rightarrow P_{1}\uplus P_{2}
by sending an element to its image under
f
. By construction,
(e_{1}\uplus e_{2})\circ e_{0}
forms a decomposition of
f
and minimality of this decomposition is immediate, as any other decomposition of
f
would contain
P_{i}
as a subpath. We obtain the following FVM theorem for the
k
variable fragment:

\displaystyle A_{1}\equiv_{{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}B_{1}\kern 5.0pt\mbox{and}\kern 5.0ptA_{2}\equiv_{{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}B_{2}

\displaystyle\mbox{implies}\quad A_{1}\uplus A_{2}\equiv_{{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}B_{1}\uplus B_{2}

The same reasoning goes through for
{\mathbb{P}_{k}}
and
{\mathbb{E}_{k}}
with coproducts over any index set
I
and for
{\mathbb{M}_{k}}
with
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R}}{{\vee}}
from Example
III.6
.

VI
Abstract FVM theorems for products

In this section we show that FVM theorems for the operation of the categorical product of two structures are automatic, regardless of the chosen comonad. This shows the power of the categorical approach since the theorem applies to any situation where logical equivalence admits a comonadic characterisation.

Recall that the universe of the product
A_{1}\times A_{2}
of two structures
A_{1},A_{2}
in category
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
consists of pairs
(a,a^{\prime})
where
a\in A_{1}
and
a^{\prime}\in A_{2}
.
For an
r
-ary relation
R\in\sigma
, the interpretation
R^{A_{1}\times A_{2}}
is defined as

R^{A_{1}\times A_{2}}\bigl((a_{1},a^{\prime}_{1}),\dots,(a_{r},a^{\prime}_{r})\bigr)

if and only if

R^{A_{1}}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r})\kern 5.0pt\mbox{and}\kern 5.0ptR^{A_{2}}(a^{\prime}_{1},\dots,a^{\prime}_{r})

This operation on the category of relational structures obeys the usual universal property of products. Namely, for any object
C
and morphisms
h_{1}\colon C\to A_{1}
and
h_{2}\colon C\to A_{2}
, there is a unique morphism
\overline{h}\colon C\to A_{1}\times A_{2}
such that
h_{i}=\pi_{i}\circ\overline{h}
, for
i=1,2
, where
\pi_{i}\colon A_{1}\times A_{2}\to A_{i}
is the
i
th projection. In case of products of relational structures,
\overline{h}
sends
c
to the pair
(h_{1}(c),h_{2}(c))
.
Products in
\mathcal{R}_{*}(\sigma)
work similarly, with the distinguished element of
(A,a)\times(B,b)
being the pair
(a,b)
.

Next, we fix an arbitrary comonad
\mathbb{C}
on
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
or
\mathcal{R}_{*}(\sigma)
.
Observe that, for objects
A_{1},A_{2}
of
\mathcal{C}
, we have morphisms

\mathbb{C}(\pi_{i})\colon\mathbb{C}(A_{1}\times A_{2})\to\mathbb{C}(A_{i})

for
i=1,2
and, by the universal property of products, also the morphism

\kappa_{A_{1},A_{2}}\colon\mathbb{C}(A_{1}\times A_{2})\to\mathbb{C}(A_{1})\times\mathbb{C}(A_{2})

Applying Theorem
III.2
immediately yields the following FVM theorem for positive existential fragments.

Proposition VI.1
.

For any comonad
\mathbb{C}
over
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
or
\mathcal{R}_{*}(\sigma)
,

\displaystyle A_{1}\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{C}}A_{2}\kern 5.0pt\mbox{and}\kern 5.0ptB_{1}\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{C}}B_{2}

\displaystyle\mbox{implies}\quad A_{1}\times A_{2}\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{C}}B_{1}\times B_{2}

A direct calculation reveals that
\kappa
is natural in the choice of
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
and, furthermore, is a Kleisli law.

Lemma VI.2
.

\kappa
is a Kleisli law.

Consequently, Theorem
IV.4
gives us an abstract FVM for counting fragments.

Proposition VI.3
.

For any comonad
\mathbb{C}
over
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
or
\mathcal{R}_{*}(\sigma)
,

\displaystyle A_{1}\equiv_{\#\mathbb{C}}A_{2}\kern 5.0pt\mbox{and}\kern 5.0ptB_{1}\equiv_{\#\mathbb{C}}B_{2}

\displaystyle\mbox{implies}\quad A_{1}\times A_{2}\equiv_{\#\mathbb{C}}B_{1}\times B_{2}

We also have an FVM theorem for products and the equivalence
\equiv_{\mathbb{C}}
.
Recall that in order to specify
\equiv_{\mathbb{C}}
we need to fix a choice of paths
\mathcal{P}
in the category
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
. As before, we assume that embeddings of coalgebras are the morphisms of coalgebras
(A,\alpha)\to(B,\beta)
whose underlying morphisms
A\to B
is an embedding of
\sigma
-structures. Similarly, we say that a morphism of coalgebras
(A,\alpha)\to(B,\beta)
is surjective if the underlying morphisms
A\to B
is surjective.

As a corollary of Theorem
V.14
, we obtain a general FVM theorem for products.
The proof proceeds by checking axioms
(S1)
and
(S2)
for the lifting of
H(A_{1},A_{2})=A_{1}\times A_{2}
, by checking the assumptions of Propositions
V.12
and
V.13
.

Theorem VI.4
.

If
\mathbb{C}
preserves embeddings and if for any surjective morphism of coalgebras
(A,\alpha)\to(B,\beta)
in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
,
if
(A,\alpha)
is a path then so is
(B,\beta)
, then

\displaystyle A_{1}\equiv_{\mathbb{C}}A_{2}\kern 5.0pt\mbox{and}\kern 5.0ptB_{1}\equiv_{\mathbb{C}}B_{2}

\displaystyle\mbox{implies}\quad A_{1}\times A_{2}\equiv_{\mathbb{C}}B_{1}\times B_{2}

Remark VI.5
.

Instead of surjective homomorphisms and embeddings we could have picked any proper factorisation system in sense of e.g.
[
45
]
on an arbitrary category with products. The same reasoning would apply.

We would like to emphasise that, as mentioned in Remark
III.3
, our abstract FVM theorems proved in this section work equally well for infinitary products.

Example VI.6
.

In the case of
{\mathbb{E}_{k}}
,
it is immediate that
{\mathbb{E}_{k}}
preserves embeddings. Furthermore, for a surjective coalgebra morphism
(P,\pi)\to(A,\alpha)
in
\mathsf{EM}({\mathbb{E}_{k}})
such that
(P,\pi)
is a path, we also have that
(A,\alpha)
is a path since, in view of Remark
V.1
, we map a finite linear order onto a forest.

The same is true for our example comonads
{\mathbb{P}_{k}}
,
{\mathbb{M}_{k}}
, yielding product FVM theorems for the bounded quantifier rank, bounded variable and bounded modal depth fragments and their positive existential and counting variants.

Example VI.7
.

More generally, the assumptions of Theorem
VI.4
hold for any comonad
\mathbb{C}
which preserves embeddings and such that the category
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
is an
arboreal category
, in sense of
[
23
]
, by Lemma 3.5 therein.

In particular, all FVM theorems for products from this section hold for
hybrid logic
because of the hybrid comonad of
[
20
]
,
guarded logics
captured by guarded comonads in
[
15
]
, the
bounded conjunction
and
bounded quantifier rank
fragments of the
k
-variable first-order logic captured in
[
17
]
and
[
18
]
, respectively.

VII
Adding equality and other enrichments

Recall that the relations
\equiv_{{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}
and
\equiv_{{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}
express logical equivalence with respect to the logic
without
equality.
The standard way to add equality to the language is to extend the signature
\sigma
with an additional binary relation symbol
I(\cdot,\cdot)
, and provide a
translation

\mathfrak{t}^{I}\colon\mathcal{R}(\sigma)\to\mathcal{R}(\sigma\cup\{I\})

which interprets this new relational symbol as equality, i.e.
\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A)
is the
\sigma\cup\{I\}
-structure extension of
A
by setting
I(a,b)
if and only if
a=b
. Since our game comonads are defined uniformly for any signature, they are also defined over
\mathcal{R}(\sigma\cup\{I\})
, giving us:

•

\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A)\equiv_{{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}\mathfrak{t}^{I}(B)
iff
A
and
B
are logically equivalent w.r.t. the fragment of first-order logic consisting of formulas of quantifier depth
{\leq}\,k
with equality, and

•

\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A)\equiv_{{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}\mathfrak{t}^{I}(B)
iff
A
and
B
are logically equivalent w.r.t. the fragment of first-order logic consisting of formulas in
k
-variables with equality.

Moreover, an operation which commutes with the translation
\mathfrak{t}^{I}
lifts FVM theorems for fragments without equality to fragments with equality. For example, disjoint unions satisfy that
\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A\uplus B)=\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A)\uplus\mathfrak{t}^{I}(B)
and, therefore, if
\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A_{1})\equiv_{{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}\mathfrak{t}^{I}(B_{1})
, and
\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A_{2})\equiv_{{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}\mathfrak{t}^{I}(B_{2})
, then

\displaystyle\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A_{1}\uplus A_{2})

\displaystyle\kern 5.0pt=\kern 5.0pt\mkern 8.0mu\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A_{1})\uplus\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A_{2})

\displaystyle\kern 5.0pt\equiv_{{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}\mathfrak{t}^{I}(B_{1})\uplus\mathfrak{t}^{I}(B_{2})=\mathfrak{t}^{I}(B_{1}\uplus B_{2})

which shows that
A_{1}\uplus A_{2}
and
B_{1}\uplus B_{2}
are logically equivalent w.r.t. formulas of quantifier depth
{\leq}\,k
with equality.

In general, similar translations can be used for other logic extensions.
For example, in
[
46
]
a translation is used to obtain description logics using
{\mathbb{M}_{k}}
.
Nevertheless, the above technique applies verbatim to varying translations, operations and logics.
We arrive at the following simple but important fact, that greatly increases the applicability of the results in the previous sections.

Theorem VII.1
.

For
i\in\{1,\dots,n+1\}
, let
\mathcal{L}_{i}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{i}
be logics and
\mathfrak{t}_{i}
a translation such that
{A\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{i}}B}
iff
{\mathfrak{t}_{i}(A)\equiv_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{i}}\mathfrak{t}_{i}(B)}
. Further, let
H^{\prime}
be an
n
-ary operation satisfying the
FVM theorem from
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{n}
to
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{n+1}
, with respect to translated structures. That is:

\mathfrak{t}_{1}(A_{1})\equiv_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1}}\mathfrak{t}_{1}(B_{1}),\;\ldots,\;\mathfrak{t}_{n}(A_{n})\equiv_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{n}}\mathfrak{t}_{n}(B_{n})

implies

H^{\prime}\bigl(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\mathfrak{t}_{i}(A_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\mathfrak{t}_{i}(A_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\mathfrak{t}_{i}(A_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{t}_{i}(A_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}}\bigr)\equiv_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{n+1}}H^{\prime}\bigl(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\mathfrak{t}_{i}(B_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\mathfrak{t}_{i}(B_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\mathfrak{t}_{i}(B_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{t}_{i}(B_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}}\bigr)

If
H
commutes with the translations in the sense that

H^{\prime}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern ,
then

satisfies the FVM theorem from
\mathcal{L}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{L}_{n}
to
\mathcal{L}_{n+1}
. That is:

A_{1}\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{1}}B_{1},\;\ldots,\;A_{n}\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{n}}B_{n}

implies

H\bigl(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern Example VII.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{t}^{\texttt{com}}\colon\mathcal{R}(\sigma)\to\mathcal{R}(\sigma^{\texttt{com}})
be a translation of
\sigma
-structures into the extension
\sigma^{\texttt{com}}
of
\sigma\cup\{I\}
with an extra binary relation
\texttt{com}(\cdot,\cdot)
. The interpretation of
com
in
\mathfrak{t}^{\texttt{com}}(A)
consists of all pairs
(a,b)
appearing in the same component of the Gaifman graph of
A
, that is, pairs
(a,b)
such that there is a path
a\leftrightsquigarrow x_{1}\leftrightsquigarrow\dots\leftrightsquigarrow x_{n}\leftrightsquigarrow b
where
x\leftrightsquigarrow y
holds whenever
x
and
y
are in a common tuple in an
R^{A}
of some
R\in\sigma
.

It is easy to see that
\mathfrak{t}^{\texttt{com}}(A)\equiv_{{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}\mathfrak{t}^{\texttt{com}}(B)
expresses logical equivalence in the bounded quantifier depth fragment extended with the connectivity predicate, akin to
[
47
,
48
]
. Since
\mathfrak{t}^{\texttt{com}}(A\uplus B)\cong\mathfrak{t}^{\texttt{com}}(A)\uplus\mathfrak{t}^{\texttt{com}}(B)
, we obtain, by Theorem
VII.1
and Example
V.15
, an FVM theorem for disjoint unions and the quantifier rank
k
fragment of said logic.
While still being polynomial-time computable, the connectivity relation is not definable in bounded quantifier depth fragments of first-order logic,
and so this is a proper extension.

Example VII.3
.

Continuing from Example
VI.6
, consider
\mathfrak{t}\colon\mathcal{R}_{*}(\sigma)\to\mathcal{R}_{*}(\sigma^{G})
where
\sigma^{G}
is the extension of the modal signature
\sigma
with the
global relation

G(a,b)
, true in
\mathfrak{t}(A,a)
for any pair of elements. Then, it is immediate that
\mathfrak{t}((A,a)\times(B,b))\cong\mathfrak{t}(A,a)\times\mathfrak{t}(B,b)
, giving us by Theorem
VII.1
an FVM theorem for products and modal logic with global modalities.

Example VII.4
.

As an example of Theorem
VII.1
where the two operations differ, we consider the notion of
weak (bi)simulation
where transitions along a distinguished relation
S\in\sigma
are considered to occur silently.
We can model weak bisimulation by considering bisimulation between translated structures where
\mathfrak{t}\colon\mathcal{R}_{*}(\sigma)\to\mathcal{R}_{*}(\sigma)
replaces all relations
R
with their closure under sequences of prior and subsequent
S
transitions.

For pointed structures
A_{1}=(A_{1},a_{1})
and
A_{2}=(A_{2},a_{2})
, there is an operation
A_{1}\vee A_{2}
which adds a new initial point
\star
to
A_{1}\uplus A_{2}
. We extend the transitions with
R(\star,(i,x))
, for
R\in\sigma
, whenever
R^{A_{i}}(a_{i},x)
for some
i\in\{1,2\}
and
x\in A_{i}
.
This construction satisfies

\mathfrak{t}(A_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S}}{{\vee}}A_{2})\cong\mathfrak{t}(A_{1})\vee\mathfrak{t}(A_{2})

by design, and there is a Kleisli law

\kappa_{A_{1},A_{2}}\colon{\mathbb{M}_{k}}(A_{1}\vee A_{2})\rightarrow{\mathbb{M}_{k}}(A_{1})\vee{\mathbb{M}_{k}}(A_{2}),

via a similar construction to that used in Example
III.6
. This yields an FVM theorem:

\displaystyle A_{1}\Rrightarrow_{W_{k}}B_{1}\kern 5.0pt\mbox{and}\kern 5.0ptA_{2}\Rrightarrow_{W_{k}}B_{2}

\displaystyle\mbox{implies}\quad A_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S}}{{\vee}}A_{2}\Rrightarrow_{W_{k}}B_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S}}{{\vee}}B_{2}

where
A\Rrightarrow_{W_{k}}B
indicates that
A
weakly simulates
B
up to depth
k
.

VIII
Conclusion

We presented a categorical approach to the composition method, and specifically Feferman–Vaught–Mostowski theorems.
We exploit game comonads to encapsulate the logics and their model comparison games.
Surprising connections to classical constructions in category theory, and especially the monad theory of bilinear maps emerged from this approach, cf. Section
V-A
.

For finite model theorists, our work provides a novel high-level account of many FVM theorems, abstracting away from individual logics and constructions.
Furthermore, concrete instances of these theorems are verified purely semantically, by finding a suitable collection homomorphisms forming a Kleisli law satisfying
(S2’)
, instead of the usual delicate verification that strategies of model comparison games compose.
For game comonads, we provide a much needed tool that enables us to handle logical relationships between structures as they are transformed or viewed in terms of different logics.

The FVM results in this paper, combined with judicious use of the techniques described in Section
VII
can be pushed significantly further.
FVM theorems and the compositional method are of particular significance in the setting of monadic second-order (MSO) logic
[
49
]
. Exploiting the results we have presented for the composition method, a follow up paper will give a comonadic semantics for MSO, and develop a semantic account of Courcelle’s algorithmic meta-theorems
[
5
]
.

The original theorem of Feferman–Vaught
[
3
]
is very flexible, including incorporating structure on the indices of families of models to be combined by an operation, as well as the models themselves. This aspect is currently outside the scope of our comonadic methods.
An ad-hoc adaptation of our approach also gives a proof of the FVM theorem for free amalgamations, but this does not follow directly from our theorems. Developing these extensions is left to future work.

The present work bears some resemblance to Turi and Plotkin’s bialgebraic semantics
[
50
]
, a categorical model of structural operational semantics (SOS)
[
51
]
.
Turi and Plotkin noticed that the SOS rules assigning behaviour to syntax could be abstracted as a certain distributive law
\lambda
.
An algebra encodes the composition operations of the syntax, and a coalgebra the behaviour.
If this pair is suitably compatible with
\lambda
, forming a so-called
\lambda
-bialgebra, then crucially bisimulation is a congruence with respect to the composition operations.
Both bialgebraic semantics and our work presented in this paper give
a categorical account of well-behaved composition operations, with the interaction between composition and observable behaviour mediated by some form of distributive law. There are also essential differences: bialgebraic semantics typically focuses on assigning behaviour to syntax,
whereas FVM theorems encompass operations on models.
Our FVM theorems are parametric in a choice of logic or observable behaviour, while the notion of bisimulation in bialgebraic semantics is fixed by the coalgebra signature functor.
The natural presence of positive existential and counting quantifier variants of FVM theorems, and the incorporation of resource parameters, do not seem to have a direct analogue in bialgebraic semantics. The similarities are intriguing, and exploring the relationship between bialgebraic semantics and our approach to FVM theorems is left to future work.

Appendix A
FVM theorems for coproducts

In this section we give a detailed account of the FVM theorems for coproducts/disjoint union discussed in Examples
III.4
,
IV.5
,
V.15
.
We explicitly prove FVM theorems for coproducts of arbitrary collection of structures with respect to logic equivalences captured by
{\mathbb{P}_{k}}
. The argument for
{\mathbb{E}_{k}}
is similar.

Recall that the (categorical) coproduct of
\coprod_{i}A_{i}
of a collection of
\sigma
-structures
\{A_{i}\}_{i\in I}
indexed by a set
I
is defined as the
\sigma
-structure with the universe being the disjoint union of the underlying universes of
A_{i}
. We an encode the universe of
\coprod_{i}A_{i}
as the set of pairs
(i,a)
such that
i\in I
and
a\in A_{i}
. For a
r
-ary relation
R\in\sigma
, the interpretation
R^{\coprod_{i}A_{i}}
is defined as

R^{\coprod_{i}A_{i}}((i_{1},a_{1}),\dots,(i_{r},a_{r}))

if and only if there exists an
i
equal to all the
i_{j}
, and

R^{A_{i}}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r})

The coproduct operation on objects of
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
extends to a functor taking collections of morphisms
f_{i}\colon A_{i}\rightarrow B_{i}
to a morphism
\coprod_{i}f_{i}\colon\coprod_{i}A_{i}\rightarrow\coprod_{i}B_{i}
where

(\coprod_{i}f_{i})(i,a)=(i,f_{i}(a))

We define a
\kappa\colon{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(\coprod_{i}A_{i})\rightarrow\coprod_{i}{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(A_{i})
as we did for the binary case in Example
III.4
.
Elements in the domain of
\kappa
are words of the form

w=\left[(p_{1},(i_{1},a_{1})),\dots,(p_{n},(i_{n},a_{n}))\right]

where for all
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
,
i_{j}\in I
and
a_{i}\in A^{i_{j}}
. We define
\kappa(w)=(i,\nu(w))
where
i=i_{n}
and
\nu(w)
is the restriction of
w
to the word of pairs
(p_{j},a_{j})
such that
i_{j}=i
.
Equivalently, when employing Haskell-style list comprehension notation
3
3

3

An expression of the form
[x_{j}\mid\varphi(j)]
applied to
s=[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]
is the restriction of
s
to only the letters
x_{j}
such that
\varphi(j)
holds.
,

\kappa(w)=\left(i,[(p_{j},a_{j})\mid i_{j}=i\;]\right).

We must show that
\kappa
as defined is a morphism of
\sigma
-structures. To check this, suppose that
R^{{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(\coprod_{i}A_{i})}(w_{1},\dots,w_{r})
for
r
-ary relation
R\in\sigma
. By condition
3
in the definition of
{\mathbb{P}_{k}}
, it holds that
R^{\coprod_{i}A_{i}}(\varepsilon(w_{1}),\dots,\varepsilon(w_{r}))
. Further from the definition of coproducts of
\sigma
-structures, there exists an
i\in I
such that all the
\varepsilon(w_{j})
are in the
i
-th component
A_{i}
of
\coprod_{i}A_{i}
.
Therefore
\kappa(w_{j})=(i,\nu(w_{j}))
for all
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.
By condition
1
in the definition of
{\mathbb{P}_{k}}
, the
w_{j}
are pairwise comparable in the prefix order. Since each
w_{j}
ends in a pebbling of an element in the
A_{i}
component, we obtain that the
\nu(w_{j})
are pairwise comparable in the prefix order.
Moreover, since the last pair of
w
is the same as the last pair of
\nu(w)
, conditions
2
and
3
in the definition of
{\mathbb{P}_{k}}
hold for
(\nu(w_{1}),\dots,\nu(w_{r}))
.
Therefore,
R^{{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(A_{i})}(\nu(w_{1}),\dots,\nu(w_{r}))
and
R^{\coprod_{i}{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(A_{i})}((i,\nu(w_{1})),\dots,(i,\nu(w_{r}))

Since the choice of the collection
\{A_{i}\}
used when defining
\kappa
was arbitrary, we obtain a collection of morphisms as required in Theorem
III.2
and, consequently an FVM theorem for coproducts and
\equiv_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}

Theorem A.1
.

Given a collections of
\sigma
-structures
\{A_{i}\}_{i\in I}
and
\{B_{i}\}_{i\in I}
indexed by a common set
I
. Then,

\textstyle(\forall i\in I.\quad A_{i}\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}B_{i})\quad\mbox{implies}\quad\coprod_{i}A_{i}\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}\coprod_{i}B_{i}.

Next, we observe that
\kappa
is natural in the choice of
\{A_{i}\}_{i}
and we show the following.

Lemma A.2
.

\kappa
is a Kleisli law.

Proof.

Axiom
(K1)
states that if the last pebbled element of
w
in
{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(\coprod_{i}A_{i})
is
a_{n}
in the
A_{i}
component of
\coprod_{i}A_{i}
, i.e.

\varepsilon(w)=(i,a),

then
a_{n}
is the last element of
\nu(w)
where
(i,\nu(w))=\kappa(w)
.
This is clear as by definition

\nu(w)=[(p_{j},a_{j})\mid i_{j}=i\;].

We have that

\kappa\circ{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(\kappa)\circ\delta(w)=\left(i,[(p_{j},\nu(w_{j}))]\mid w_{j}\in S_{i}(w)]\right)

where
S_{i}(w)
is the set of prefixes of
w
in
{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(\coprod A_{i})
which end in pebbling an element of
A_{i}
.
Axiom
(K2)
states that taking prefixes of the restriction
\nu(w)
of
w
to letters in the component
A_{i}
is the same as listing the
A_{i}
component restrictions
\nu(w_{j})
of each of the prefixes
w_{j}
in
S_{i}(w)
.
This is clear by inspection.
∎

Since
\kappa
is a Kleisli law, we can apply Theorem
IV.4
to obtain the FVM theorem for coproducts and the
k
-variable counting fragment.

Theorem A.3
.

Given the same assumptions as in Theorem
A.1
,

\textstyle(\forall i\in I.\quad A_{i}\equiv_{\#{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}B_{i})\quad\mbox{implies}\quad\coprod_{i}A_{i}\equiv_{\#{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}\coprod_{i}B_{i}.

Lemma A.4
.

The operation
\coprod_{i}
preserves embeddings.

Proof.

Consider the collection of embeddings
\{f_{i}\colon A_{i}\rightarrowtail B_{i}\}
indexed by
I
.

To see that
\coprod_{i}f_{i}
is injective, suppose

(\coprod_{i}f_{i})(l,a)=(\coprod_{i}f_{i})(j,a^{\prime}).

Since
(\coprod_{i}f_{i})(l,a)
is equal to
(l,f_{l}(a))
, we have that
l=j
and
(l,f_{l}(a))=(l,f_{l}(a^{\prime}))
. By injectivity of
f_{l}
,
a=a^{\prime}
, so
(l,a)=(j,a^{\prime})
and
\coprod_{i}f_{i}
is injective.

To see that
\coprod_{i}f_{i}
reflects relations, suppose that

R^{\coprod_{i}A_{i}}((i_{1},a_{1}),\dots,(i_{r},a_{r}))
, then:

\displaystyle R^{\coprod_{i}A_{i}}((i_{1},a_{1}),\dots,(i_{r},a_{r}))

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow R^{A_{j}}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r})

for some
j\in I
with
j=i_{1}=\dots=i_{r}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow R^{B_{j}}\left(f_{j}(a_{1}),\dots,f_{j}(a_{r})\right)\text{ $f_{j}$ is an embedding}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow R^{\coprod_{i}B_{i}}\left((j,f_{j}(a_{1})),\dots,(j,f_{j}(a_{r}))\right)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow R^{\coprod_{i}B_{i}}\left((i_{1},f_{j}(a_{1})),\dots,(i_{r},f_{j}(a_{r}))\right)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow R^{\coprod_{i}B_{i}}\left(\coprod_{i}f_{i}(i_{1},a_{1}),\dots,\coprod_{i}f_{i}(i_{r},a_{r})\right)

∎

Lemma A.5
.

For any path
(P,\pi)
in
\mathsf{EM}({\mathbb{P}_{k}})
and coalgebras
(A_{i},\alpha_{i})
in
\mathsf{EM}({\mathbb{P}_{k}})
, for
i\in I
, every morphism
f\colon P\to\coprod_{i}A_{i}
which makes the following diagram commute

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coprod_{i}{A_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(\coprod_{i}A_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coprod_{i}{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(A_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coprod_{i}\alpha_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}

has a minimal decomposition through
\coprod_{i}e_{i}\colon\coprod_{i}P_{i}\to\coprod_{i}A_{i}
, for some path embeddings
e_{i}\colon(P_{i},\pi_{i})\rightarrowtail(A_{i},\alpha_{i})
, with
i\in I
.

Proof.

We need to define a decomposition of
f
consisting of

P\xrightarrow{e_{0}}\coprod_{i}P_{i}\xrightarrow{\coprod_{i}e_{i}}\coprod_{i}A_{i}

where
e_{i}\colon(P_{i},\pi_{i})\rightarrowtail(A_{i},\alpha_{i})
are embeddings of coalgebras.
Unpacking the diagram that
f
satisfies, the image of
P
under
{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(f)\circ\pi
contains the words
w
such that for every
i\in I
, the restriction
\nu(w)
by
\kappa
to the
A_{i}
component yields a word in the image of
\alpha_{i}
.
This allows us to take
P_{i}
to be the image
f(x)
of
x\in P
which yields a path with respect to
\sqsubseteq_{\alpha_{i}}
in
A_{i}
.
Since
P_{i}
is a substructure of
A_{i}
, we obtain embeddings
e_{i}\colon(P_{i},\pi_{i})\rightarrowtail(A_{i},\alpha_{i})

and
e_{0}\colon P\rightarrow\coprod_{i}P_{i}
sends
x
to
(i,f(x))
whenever
f(x)
is in the
A_{i}
component.
Clearly, by construction
f=(\coprod_{i}e_{i})\circ e_{0}
.
The minimality of this decomposition is immediate, as any other decomposition of
f=(\coprod_{i}m_{i})\circ m_{0}
would have to factor through the image of
f
in
A_{i}
, i.e.
P_{i}
, as a subpath.
∎

The above lemmas show that
\coprod_{i}
and
\kappa
satisfy the conditions of Theorem
V.14
, so we obtain:

Theorem A.6
.

Given the same assumptions as in Theorem
A.1
,

\textstyle(\forall i\in I.\quad A_{i}\equiv_{{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}B_{i})\quad\mbox{implies}\quad\coprod_{i}A_{i}\equiv_{{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}\coprod_{i}B_{i}.

A similar
\kappa\colon{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(\coprod_{i}A_{i})\rightarrow\coprod_{i}{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(A_{i})
exists satisfying the necessary requirements and demonstrating that
\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}
,
\equiv_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}
,
\equiv_{\#{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}
, and
\equiv_{{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}
are preserved by taking arbitrary coproducts of
\sigma
-structures.

Appendix B
Proofs omitted from Section
III

See
III.2

Proof.

Let
f_{i}\colon\mathbb{C}_{i}(A_{i})\to B_{i}
be the morphism witnessing the relation
A_{i}\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{C}_{i}}B_{i}
, for
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
. Since
H
is a functor, the morphism
\overline{f}
defined as the composition

\mathbb{D}(H(A_{1},\dots,A_{n}))\xrightarrow{H(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})\circ\kappa_{A_{1},\dots,A_{n}}}H(B_{1},\dots,B_{n})

witnesses the relation
H(A_{1},\dots,A_{n})\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{D}}H(B_{1},\dots,B_{n})
.

The corresponding statement for
\equiv_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{C}}
is obtained by applying the same argument also to the collection of morphisms
g_{i}\colon\mathbb{C}_{i}(B_{i})\to A_{i}
that witness
B_{i}\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{C}_{i}}A_{i}
, for
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.
∎

Appendix C
Proofs omitted from Section
IV

We first prove a generalisation of Lemma
IV.1
. Assume that
H\colon\mathcal{C}_{1}\times\dots\times\mathcal{C}_{n}\to\mathcal{D}
is an
n
-ary functor and
\kappa
is a collection of morphisms

\displaystyle\{

\displaystyle\,\mathbb{D}(H(A_{1},\dots,A_{n}))\xrightarrow{\kappa_{A_{1},\dots,A_{n}}}H(\mathbb{C}_{1}(A_{1}),\dots,\mathbb{C}_{n}(A_{n}))\mid

\displaystyle A_{1}\in\mathcal{C}_{1},\,\dots,\,A_{n}\in\mathcal{C}_{n}\}.

Lemma C.1
.

Given
H
and
\kappa
satisfying
(K1)
, the following are equivalent:

•

For every tuple of morphisms

f_{1}\colon\mathbb{C}_{1}(A_{1})\to B_{1},\ \dots,\ f_{n}\colon\mathbb{C}_{n}(A_{n})\to B_{n}

we have

\displaystyle H(f_{1}^{*},\dots,f_{n}^{*})\circ\kappa_{A_{1},\dots,A_{n}}=

(9)

\displaystyle\kappa_{B_{1},\dots,B_{n}}\circ(H(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})\circ\kappa_{A_{1},\dots,A_{n}})^{*}

•

\kappa
is natural in
A_{1},\dots,A_{n}
and satisfies
(K2)
.

Proof.

To start with, let us recall some standard facts about coextension that we use without mentioning, and which are true for any comonad
(\mathbb{C},\varepsilon,(-)^{*})
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(f)

\displaystyle=(\mathbb{C}(A)\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}A\xrightarrow{f}B)^{*}

\displaystyle(\forall f\colon A\to B)

\displaystyle\delta_{A}

\displaystyle=(\mathbb{C}(A)\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{C}(A)}}\mathbb{C}(A))^{*}

\displaystyle(\forall A\in\mathcal{C})

\displaystyle f^{*}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{C}(A)\xrightarrow{\delta_{A}}\mathbb{C}^{2}(A)\xrightarrow{\mathbb{C}(f)}\mathbb{C}(B)

\displaystyle(\forall f\colon\mathbb{C}(A)\to B)

First, we show that (
9
) implies that
\kappa
is natural, that is, we check the following

\displaystyle H(\mathbb{C}_{1}(g_{1}),\dots,\mathbb{C}_{n}(g_{n}))\circ\kappa_{A_{1},\dots,A_{n}}=

\displaystyle\kappa_{B_{1},\dots,B_{n}}\circ\mathbb{D}(H(g_{1},\dots,g_{n}))

for any choice of
g_{1}\colon A_{1}\to B_{1},\,\dots,\,g_{n}\colon A_{n}\to B_{n}
. For
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
define

f_{i}=\mathbb{C}_{i}(A_{i})\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}A_{i}\xrightarrow{g_{i}}B_{i},

then it follows that

\displaystyle H(\mathbb{C}_{1}(g_{1}),\dots,\mathbb{C}_{n}(g_{n}))

\displaystyle=H(f_{1}^{*},\dots,f_{n}^{*})

and similarly, with the vector notation introduced in Section
IV-A
,

Consequently, (
9
) directly entails naturality of
\kappa
.
Similarly, to check
(K2)
, for
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
we set

f_{i}=\mathbb{C}_{i}(A_{i})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}}\mathbb{C}_{i}(A_{i}).

0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\delta_{A_{i}}\hfil$\crcr}}}\bigr)\circ\kappa.

Lastly, we show that from naturality of
\kappa
and
(K2)
we obtain that (
9
) holds for any choice of morphisms

f_{1}\colon\mathbb{C}_{1}(A_{1})\to B_{1},\,\dots,\,f_{n}\colon\mathbb{C}_{n}(A_{n})\to B_{n}.

The desired equality follows by a direct computation:

definition

∎

See
IV.4

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle f_{1}\colon\mathbb{C}_{1}(A_{1})

\displaystyle\to B_{1}

\displaystyle f_{n}\colon\mathbb{C}_{n}(A_{n})

\displaystyle\to B_{n}

and

\displaystyle g_{1}\colon\mathbb{C}_{1}(B_{1})

\displaystyle\to A_{1}

\displaystyle g_{n}\colon\mathbb{C}_{n}(B_{n})

\displaystyle\to A_{n}

be the pairs of morphisms witnessing

A_{1}\equiv_{\#\mathbb{C}_{1}}B_{1},\,\dots,\,A_{n}\equiv_{\#\mathbb{C}_{n}}B_{n},

respectively. This means that, for every
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, the morphisms
f_{i},g_{i}
are inverse to each other in
\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{C}_{i})
, i.e. that
g_{i}\mathbin{\bullet}f_{i}=\varepsilon_{A_{i}}
and
f_{i}\mathbin{\bullet}g_{i}=\varepsilon_{B_{i}}
.

We wish to show that

\mathbb{D}(H(A_{1},\dots,A_{n}))\xrightarrow{H(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})\circ\kappa_{A_{1},\dots,A_{n}}}H(B_{1},\dots,B_{n})

and

\mathbb{D}(H(B_{1},\dots,B_{n}))\xrightarrow{H(g_{1},\dots,g_{n})\circ\kappa_{B_{1},\dots,B_{n}}}H(A_{1},\dots,A_{n})

are inverse to each other in
\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{D})
.
We follow exactly the same lines as for the unary case to show this.
By Lemma
C.1
we can assume that (
9
) holds for
\kappa
. Therefore, we have:

\displaystyle\bigl(H(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern .
∎

Note that a proof of the previous fact can be carried without invoking Lemma
C.1
, straight from axioms
(K1)
and
(K2)
.

Appendix D
Proofs omitted from Section
V

D-A

Proofs omitted from Section
V-A

Recall that a morphism
e\colon E\to A
is an
equaliser
of a pair of morphisms
f\colon A\to B
and
g\colon A\to B
if
f\circ e=g\circ e
and, for any other
h\colon C\to A
such that
f\circ h=g\circ h
, there is a unique
\overline{h}\colon C\to E
such that
e\circ\overline{h}=h
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{h}}

Equalisers do not need to exist in a given category but they do in our main examples, that is, in categories
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
and
\mathcal{R}_{*}(\sigma)
.
Namely, the equaliser of
\sigma
-structure homomorphisms
f,g\colon A\to B
is the inclusion of the induced substructure on
\{x\in A\mid f(x)=g(x)\}
into
A
.
Moreover, the following standard fact from category theory ensures that we also often have equalisers in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
.

Lemma D.1
.

If a comonad
\mathbb{C}
on
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
or on
\mathcal{R}_{*}(\sigma)
preserves embeddings then
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
has equalisers.

Proof.

Linton’s theorem
[
39
]
says that
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
has equalisers if
\mathcal{C}
has coproducts, a proper factorisation system
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
, is
\mathcal{M}
-well-powered and
\mathbb{C}
preserves
\mathcal{M}
-morphisms. We check that this is true in case when
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
and
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})=(\text{surjective homomorphisms},\text{embeddings})
:

•

\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
has all coproducts, given by taking disjoint unions of any collection of
\sigma
-structures. Coproducts in
\mathcal{R}_{*}(\sigma)
are the disjoint unions glued at the designated points.

•

\mathcal{M}
-well-powered requires that no object has a proper class of non-isomorphic substructures but this trivially holds about (pointed)
\sigma
-structures as every structure
A
has a bounded number of substructures, depending on the cardinality of
A
.

•

Lastly, the fact that (surjective homomorphisms, embeddings) forms a proper factorisation system follows abstractly from the fact that surjective homomorphisms and embeddings are precisely epimorphisms and extremal monomorphisms, respectively, and that
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
and
\mathcal{R}_{*}(\sigma)
are complete wellpowered categories and as such (epi, extremal mono) is a factorisation system, see e.g.
[
52
]
.
For a direct proof, just observe that every homomorphism of (pointed)
\sigma
-structures
f\colon A\to B
factors as
m\circ q
where
q
is a surjective homomorphism and
m
is an embedding. It is immediate that this decomposition satisfies the uniqueness properties required of factorisation systems in sense of
[
45
]
.
∎

Note that the above proof would work even more generally, e.g. for categories with multiple designated points, so long the assumptions of Linton’s theorem are satisfied.

We now come back to our main task. We wish to “lift” an
n
-ary functor
H\colon\prod_{i}\mathcal{C}_{i}\to\mathcal{D}
to an operation between the categories of coalgebras:

\widehat{H}\colon\prod_{i}\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C}_{i})\to\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})

We assume we have a Kleisli law
\kappa\colon\mathbb{D}\circ H\Rightarrow H\circ\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}_{i}
and that
\mathbb{D}
preserves embeddings. Then, for every for coalgebras
(A_{1},\alpha_{1})\in\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C}_{1})
, …,
(A_{n},\alpha_{n})\in\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C}_{n})
, the following equaliser exists by Lemma
D.1
.

Remark D.2
.

, which we denote by

∎

Bimorphisms

Before proving Proposition
V.4
, we introduce an instructive terminology and notation. Given coalgebras

(A,\alpha)\in\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D}),\,(B_{1},\beta_{1})\in\mathsf{EM}(C_{1}),\,\dots,\,(B_{n},\beta_{n})\in\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C}_{n}),

we say that a morphism

g\colon A\to H(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle B_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle B_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle B_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle B_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}})

is a
bimorphism

g\colon\alpha\to[\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\beta_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.

2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\beta_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\beta_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}}
(Lemma
D.4
.2). On the other hand, for a bimorphism
g\colon\alpha\to\left[\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\beta_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\beta_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\beta_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\beta_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}\right]
we clearly have
(g^{o})^{\#}=g
, again by the universal property of
\mathrm{u}_{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\beta_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\beta_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\beta_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\beta_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}}
. This establishes the required bijection between coalgebra morphisms and bimorphisms.

where the equality in the middle follows from Lemma

.3. We see that

now follows from these two observations and Lemma

, which ensures that

is a bimorphism.

D-B

Proofs omitted from Section
V-C

Recall that a functor
R\colon\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{S}
has a left adjoint if and only if, for every object
b\in\mathcal{S}
, there is an object
L(b)
in
\mathcal{T}
and a morphism
\eta_{b}\colon b\to R(L(b))
such that, for any other
f\colon b\to R(a)
there is a unique
f^{*}\colon L(b)\to a
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(L(b))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(f^{*})}

A similar statement holds for relative adjoints too, with a proof that follows essentially the same steps.

Proposition D.5
.

Let
R\colon\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{U}
and
I\colon\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{U}
be functors and further assume that there is

•

a mapping on objects
L\colon\mathrm{obj}(\mathcal{S})\to\mathrm{obj}(\mathcal{T})
and

•

a morphism
\eta_{a}\colon I(a)\to RL(a)
, for every
a\in\mathcal{S}
, such that for any
f\colon I(a)\to R(b)
there is a unique
{\overline{f}\colon L(a)\to b}
such that the following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces RL(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}}

Then, the mapping
L
extends to a functor
L\colon\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{T}
and there is a natural bijection

\rho:\hom(L(A),B)\xrightarrow{\kern 4.09723pt\cong\kern 4.09723pt}\hom(I(A),R(B)).

witnessing that
R
is a relative right adjoint.

Proof.

We first observe that
L
extends to a functor. Given
h\colon a\to a^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{S}
, we set
L(h)
to be the morphism
L(a)\to L(b)
obtained by the universal property of
\eta_{a}
for the morphism

I(a)\xrightarrow{I(h)}I(a^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\eta_{a^{\prime}}}RL(a^{\prime}).

As a by-product we also obtain that
\eta
is natural in
a
.

Next, we define
\rho
as follows:

\displaystyle L(a)\xrightarrow{z}b

\displaystyle\quad\overset{\rho}{\longmapsto}\quad I(a)\xrightarrow{\eta_{a}}RL(a)\xrightarrow{R(z)}R(b)

\displaystyle I(a)\xrightarrow{f}R(b)

\displaystyle\quad\overset{\rho^{-1}}{\longmapsto}\quad L(a)\xrightarrow{\overline{f}}b

where
\overline{f}
is the morphism obtained by the universal property of
\eta_{a}
.

It remains to check that
\rho
is natural and componentwise bijective. For the latter, let
z
be a morphism
L(A)\to b
in
\mathcal{T}
. Then,
\rho^{-1}(\rho(z))=\rho^{-1}(R(z)\circ\eta_{e})=\overline{R(z)\circ\eta_{e}}
. By the universal property of
\eta_{a}
, it is the unique
s\colon L(a)\to b
such that
R(s)\circ\eta_{a}=R(z)\circ\eta_{a}
. But this equation is already solved by
s=z
, hence
\rho^{-1}(\rho(z))=z
. Conversely, for an
f
of the form
I(a)\to R(b)
,
\rho(\rho^{-1}(f))=R(\overline{f})\circ\eta_{a}
which is equal to
f
, as required.

For naturality of
\rho
, let
h\colon a^{\prime}\to a
and
g\colon b\to b^{\prime}
be morphisms in
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{T}
, respectively. For commutativity of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom(L(a),b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom(I(a),R(b))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom(L(a^{\prime}),b^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom(I(a^{\prime}),R(b^{\prime}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom(L(h),g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom(I(h),R(g))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

let
f
be a morphism in the top left set, i.e.
f\colon L(a)\to b
. Following the right-to-down path,
f
is mapped to

I(a^{\prime})\xrightarrow{I(h)}I(a)\xrightarrow{\eta_{a}}RL(a)\xrightarrow{R(f)}R(b)\xrightarrow{R(g)}R(b^{\prime})

and, following the down-to-right path,
f
is mapped to

I(a^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\eta_{a^{\prime}}}RL(a^{\prime})\xrightarrow{R(L(h))}RL(a)\xrightarrow{R(f)}R(b)\xrightarrow{R(g)}R(b^{\prime}).

However, these two morphism are equal by naturality of
\eta
.
∎
