Proof.

(\Rightarrow)
Assuming (S2) and fixing
D\in\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})
, we define the relative left adjoint
L_{D}\colon\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}\widehat{H}(D)\to\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{D}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}D
of
\widehat{H}_{D}
. Following Proposition
D.5
, it is enough to define the appropriate mapping on objects

L_{D}\colon\mathrm{obj}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}\widehat{H}(D))\to\mathrm{obj}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{D}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}D)

and a collection of morphisms
\eta_{e}\colon I(e)\to\widehat{H}_{D}L_{D}(e)
, for every object
e
of
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}\widehat{H}(D)
i.e. an embedding
e\colon\pi\rightarrowtail\widehat{H}(D)
in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
.

Given
e\colon\pi\rightarrowtail\widehat{H}(D)
, we denote by

\pi\xrightarrow{\kern 4.09723ptg_{e}\kern 4.09723pt}\widehat{H}(\pi_{e})\xrightarrow{\widehat{H}(h_{e})}\widehat{H}(D)

its minimal decomposition. Define
L_{D}(e)
as
h_{e}
and
\eta_{e}
as
g_{e}
. The fact that
g_{e}
with
\widehat{H}(h_{e})
is a decomposition of
e
expresses the fact that
\eta_{e}
is indeed a morphism
I(e)\to\widehat{H}_{D}(L_{D}(e))
in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}\widehat{H}(D)
, that is, we have the following.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}(\pi_{e})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}(D)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}(L_{D}(e))}

(12)

Next, we check the universal property of
\eta_{e}
. Any morphism
z\colon I(e)\to\widehat{H}_{D}(g)
in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}\widehat{H}(D)
, by definition, corresponds to having a commutative triangle in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}(\rho)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}(D)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}(g)}

for a path embedding
g\colon\rho\rightarrowtail D
. Consequently, we have a commutative square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}(\pi_{e})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}(\rho)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}(D)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}(h_{e})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}(g)}

By minimality of the decomposition (
12
), there is a morphism
z^{*}\colon\pi_{e}\to\rho
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

i.e.
z^{*}
is a morphism
\pi_{e}\to\rho
in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{D}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}D
. Furthermore, by (S1),
\widehat{H}
preserves embeddings. Hence,
\widehat{H}(g)
is a monomorphism and the previous two diagrams give us that
z=\widehat{H}(z^{*})\circ g_{e}
. In other words, the following diagram commutes in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}\widehat{H}(D)
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I(e)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}_{D}(L_{D}(e))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}_{D}(g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}_{D}(z^{*})}

Unicity of
z^{*}
follows from the fact that
g
is a monomorphism.

(\Leftarrow)
Assume
L_{D}
is the relative left adjoint of
\widehat{H}_{D}
from
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{D}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}D
to the embedding functor
I\colon\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}\widehat{H}(D)\hookrightarrow\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}\widehat{H}(D)
, for a fixed
a\in\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})
, and let
\tau
be the corresponding natural bijection

\tau\colon\hom(L_{D}(u),e)\xrightarrow{\kern 4.09723pt\cong\kern 4.09723pt}\hom(I(u),\widehat{H}_{D}(e)).

Next, given a path embedding
e\colon\pi\rightarrowtail\widehat{H}(D)
, observe that it is an object of
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}\widehat{H}(D)
. Set

e^{\prime}\colon\pi^{\prime}\rightarrowtail D\quad\mbox{and}\quad e_{0}\colon\pi\to\widehat{H}(\pi^{\prime})

to be
L_{D}(e)
and the underlying morphism of the unit
\eta_{e}\colon I(e)\to\widehat{H}_{D}L_{D}(e)
of the relative adjunction, respectively, i.e.
e_{0}=\tau(\mathrm{id}\colon L_{D}(e)\to L_{D}(e))
. Observe that, the fact that
\eta_{e}
is a morphism in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}\widehat{H}(D)
says precisely that
e
is equal to the composition

\pi\xrightarrow{\kern 4.09723pte_{0}\kern 4.09723pt}\widehat{H}(\pi^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\widehat{H}(L_{D}(e))}\widehat{H}(D).

For minimality of this decomposition, consider an alternative decomposition of
e

\pi\xrightarrow{\kern 4.09723ptg_{0}\kern 4.09723pt}\widehat{H}(\rho)\xrightarrow{\widehat{H}(g_{1})}\widehat{H}(D)

where
g_{1}
is a path embedding
g_{1}\colon\rho\rightarrowtail D
in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})
. The fact that it is a decomposition of
e
expresses the fact that
g_{0}
is a morphism
I(e)\to\widehat{H}_{D}(g_{1})
in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}\widehat{H}(D)
. Therefore, the morphism
\tau^{-1}(g_{0})
gives
L_{D}(e)\to g_{1}
in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{D}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}D
. In other words, the underlying morphism of coalgebras of
\tau^{-1}(g_{0})
is of type
\pi^{\prime}\to\rho
and
e^{\prime}
is equal to
g_{1}\circ\tau^{-1}(g_{0})
in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})
.
∎

D-C

Proofs omitted from Section
V-D

We need the following standard fact about equalisers.

Lemma D.6
.

Assume that
\mathbb{C}
is a comonad on the category of (pointed) relational structures which preserves embeddings. If
\iota\colon E\to A
is an equaliser of a pair of morphisms
A\rightrightarrows B
in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
then
\iota
is an embedding.

Proof.

Recall that the factorisation of maps into surjective homomorphisms followed by embeddings lifts to the category
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
, cf. Lemma
E.4
. In fact, this is a factorisation system
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
with
\mathcal{E}\subseteq\text{epis}
and
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\text{monos}
, in sense of
[
45
]
. It then follows that embeddings in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
contain all equalisers, see e.g. Proposition 2.1.4 in
[
45
]
.
∎

Proof.

e_{1}\colon(A_{1},\alpha_{1})\to(B_{1},\beta_{1}),\,\dots,\,e_{n}\colon(A_{n},\alpha_{n})\to(B_{n},\beta_{n})

, respectively. Recall that

is an embedding as well because

preserves embeddings. Furthermore, by Lemma

,

∎

Recall the definition of bimorphisms and the notation we introduced for them in Section
D-A
.

Lemma D.7
.

Assume that both
\mathbb{D}
and
H
preserve embeddings.
Then, given a bimorphism
f\colon\alpha\to[\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\beta_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern Since
f
is a bimorphism, we know that the outer square commutes. Further, the two squares on the right commute by naturality of
\kappa
and the assumption that
e_{i}
, for
1\leq i\leq n
, is a coalgebra morphism. A simple diagram chasing implies that

and

preserve embeddings,
H(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern is an embedding and hence a mono (cf. Lemma

.1), proving that
f_{0}
is a bimorphism.
∎

Proof.

, there exist coalgebra morphisms

See
V.14

Proof.

Given a collection of open pathwise-embeddings

\displaystyle F^{\mathbb{C}_{1}}(A_{1})\xleftarrow{f_{1}}(R_{1},

\displaystyle\rho_{1})\xrightarrow{g_{1}}F^{\mathbb{C}_{1}}(B_{1})

\displaystyle F^{\mathbb{C}_{n}}(A_{n})\xleftarrow{f_{n}}(R_{n},

\displaystyle\rho_{n})\xrightarrow{g_{n}}F^{\mathbb{C}_{n}}(B_{n})

in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C}_{1}),\dots,\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C}_{n})
, respectively, we wish to construct a pair of open pathwise embeddings

\displaystyle F^{\mathbb{D}}(H(A_{1},\dots,A_{n}))\xleftarrow{f}(R,\rho)\xrightarrow{g}F^{\mathbb{D}}(H(B_{1},\dots,B_{n}))

(13)

in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})
.

Since there is a Kleisli law
\kappa\colon\mathbb{D}\circ H\Rightarrow H\circ\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}_{i}
, we know by Theorem
V.3
that
H
lifts to
\widehat{H}\colon\prod_{i}\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C}_{i})\to\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})
.
Also, since
H
preserves embeddings, we know that
(S1)
holds by Proposition
V.12
.
Furthermore,
(S2)
holds too by Proposition
V.13
because we assumed that
\kappa
satisfies
(S2’)
.
Consequently, by Theorem
V.10
,
\widehat{H}
sends tuples of open pathwise-embeddings to open pathwise-embeddings.

We obtain a pair of open pathwise-embeddings
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}(F^{\mathbb{C}_{1}}(A_{1}),\dots,F^{\mathbb{C}_{n}}(A_{n}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle(R_{i},\rho_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}{\vbox{\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle(R_{i},\rho_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}{\vbox{\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle(R_{i},\rho_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}{\vbox{\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle(R_{i},\rho_{i})\hfil$\crcr}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}(F^{\mathbb{C}_{1}}(B_{1}),\dots,F^{\mathbb{C}_{n}}(B_{n}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}(g_{1},\dots,g_{n})}

as the morphisms of coalgebras
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}
and
g_{1},\dots,g_{n}
are open pathwise-embeddings too.

Finally, we recall that, by Theorem
V.3
,

F^{\mathbb{D}}(H(A_{1},\dots,A_{n}))\cong\widehat{H}(F^{\mathbb{C}_{1}}(A_{1}),\dots,F^{\mathbb{C}_{n}}(A_{n}))

and

F^{\mathbb{D}}(H(B_{1},\dots,B_{n}))\cong\widehat{H}(F^{\mathbb{C}_{1}}(B_{1}),\dots,F^{\mathbb{C}_{n}}(B_{n})).

This gives us that
\widehat{H}(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})
and
\widehat{H}(g_{1},\dots,g_{n})
are of the required type, as in (
13
).
∎

Appendix E
Proofs omitted from Section
VI

Here we show FVM theorems for products of arbitrary collection of structures. Note that Section
VI
is stated in terms of binary products only but here we work with infinitary products, to support the claim preceding Example
VI.6
.

As with binary products, the FVM theorems for the
\equiv_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{C}}
and
\equiv_{\#\mathbb{C}}
relations we do not need to require anything about the comonad
\mathbb{C}
on
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
. For the FVM theorem for
\equiv_{\mathbb{C}}
we need to have that
\mathbb{C}
preserves embeddings and assure that codomains of surjective morphisms from paths are paths as well, in the category
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
.

Recall that the (categorical) product
\prod_{i}A_{i}
of a collection of
\sigma
-structures
\{A_{i}\}_{i\in I}
, indexed by a set
I
, is defined as the structure with the universe being the product of the underlying universes
\prod_{i}A_{i}
. Further, for a relation
R
in
\sigma
, define its realisation in the product by

R^{\prod_{i}A_{i}}(\overline{a}_{1},\dots,\overline{a}_{n})\iff(\forall i)\quad R^{A_{i}}(a_{1,i},\,\dots,\,a_{n,i})

Recall that the product of a collection objects
\{A_{i}\}_{i\in I}
in a category is given by an object
\prod_{i}A_{i}
and a collection of projection morphisms
\pi_{i}\colon\prod_{i}A_{i}\to A_{i}
which are
universal
with this property. This means that for any other object
B
and a collection of morphisms
f_{i}\colon B\to A_{i}
there is a unique morphism
\overline{f}\colon B\to\prod_{i}A_{i}
such that
f_{i}=\pi_{i}\circ\overline{f}
, for every
i\in I
.

Observe that for any product
\prod_{i}A_{i}
, we have a collection of maps

\mathbb{C}\left(\prod\nolimits_{i}A_{i}\right)\xrightarrow{\mathbb{C}\left(\pi_{i}\right)}\mathbb{C}(A_{i})\qquad(\forall i)

therefore, by universality, there exists a unique morphism

\textstyle\kappa\colon\mathbb{C}\left(\prod_{i}A_{i}\right)\to\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}(A_{i}).

Since the choice of
\{A_{i}\}_{i}
used in defining
\kappa
was arbitrary, we obtain a collection of morphisms as required in Theorem
III.2
and, consequently, an FVM theorem for products and
\equiv_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{C}}
.

Theorem E.1
.

Given collections of
\sigma
-structures
\{A_{i}\}_{i\in I}
and
\{B_{i}\}_{i\in I}
, indexed by a common set
I
, and a comonad
\mathbb{C}
on
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
. Then,

\textstyle\left(\forall i\in I.\;A_{i}\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{C}_{i}}B_{i}\right)\quad\mbox{implies}\quad\prod_{i}A_{i}\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{C}_{i}}\prod_{i}B_{i}.

Next, we observe that
\kappa
is natural in the choice of
\{A_{i}\}_{i}
and we can also show the following.

Lemma E.2
.

\kappa
is a Kleisli law.

Proof.

First, observe that by the definition of products and how
\kappa
is defined, we have that

\mathbb{C}(\pi_{i})=\pi_{i}\circ\kappa

(14)

for every
i
. From this we show commutativity of the following diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}\left(\prod_{i}A_{i}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}(A_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{i}}

By universality of the products, this implies the counit axiom of Kleisli laws that is
(K1)
for the operation
H(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}})=\prod_{i}A_{i}
. To show commutativity of the pentagon above, compute:

\displaystyle\pi_{i}\circ\left(\prod\nolimits_{i}\varepsilon\right)\circ\kappa

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\circ\pi_{i}\circ\kappa

products

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\circ\mathbb{C}(\pi_{i})

(
14
)

\displaystyle=\pi_{i}\circ\varepsilon

naturality

Next, to show
(K2)
for the operation
H(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}})=\prod_{i}A_{i}
, we employ the same strategy. We wish to show commutativity of the following diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(\prod_{i}A_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}(A_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}^{2}(\prod_{i}A_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}(A_{i}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}^{2}(A_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}^{2}(A_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}^{2}(A_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(\kappa)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{i}}

To do so, we compute:

\displaystyle\pi_{i}\circ\left(\prod\nolimits_{i}\delta\right)\circ\kappa

\displaystyle=\delta\circ\pi_{i}\circ\kappa

products

\displaystyle=\delta\circ\mathbb{C}(\pi_{i})

(
14
)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{C}^{2}(\pi_{i})\circ\delta

naturality

\displaystyle=\mathbb{C}(\pi_{i})\circ\mathbb{C}(\kappa)\circ\delta

(
14
)

\displaystyle=\pi_{i}\circ\kappa\circ\mathbb{C}(\kappa)\circ\delta

(
14
)

∎

As a corollary of this fact and Theorem
IV.4
, we obtain the FVM theorem for products and the counting fragment.

Theorem E.3
.

Given the same assumptions as in Theorem
E.1
,

\textstyle\left(\forall i\in I.\;A_{i}\equiv_{\#\mathbb{C}}B_{i}\right)\quad\mbox{implies}\quad\prod_{i}A_{i}\equiv_{\#\mathbb{C}}\prod_{i}B_{i}.

Lastly, we show the FVM theorem for products and the equivalence
\equiv_{\mathbb{C}}
.
As before, we assume we have fixed a collection of paths
\mathcal{P}
in the category
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
in order to specify
\equiv_{\mathbb{C}}
. Surjective morhisms and embeddings in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
are defined as usual.

We need to do a little bit of preliminary work before proving the FVM theorem. First, recall that every homomorphism of
\sigma
-structures
f\colon A\to B
factors as

A\xrightarrow{q}f[A]\xrightarrow{e}B

where
f[A]
is the substructure of
B
induced by the image of
A
under
f
. Importantly, the second map
e
is an embedding and
q
is just a surjective homomorphism.

It is a standard categorical fact that this factorisation of homomorphisms lifts to the category
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
if our comonad preserves embeddings.

Lemma E.4
.

Assume that
\mathbb{C}
preserves embeddings and that
f\colon(A,\alpha)\to(B,\beta)
is a morphism of
\mathbb{C}
-coalgebras. Then,
f
factorises as

(A,\alpha)\xrightarrow{q}(f[A],\beta^{\prime})\xrightarrow{e}(B,\beta)

for some coalgebra
(f[A],\beta^{\prime})
, where
q\colon A\to f[A]
and
e\colon f[A]\rightarrowtail B
is the factorisation of the homomorphism of
\sigma
-structures
f\colon A\to B
as above.

Proof.

Assume that
f\colon A\to B
is factored as
e\circ q
as above. This means that we have the solid arrows in the following diagram commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f[A]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(f[A])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(e)}

The following well-known fact and also our assumption that
\mathbb{C}
preserves embeddings justifies that there exists a morphism
\beta^{\prime}\colon f[A]\to\mathbb{C}(f[A])
which makes the whole diagram above commute.

Claim E.5
.

In
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
or
\mathcal{R}_{*}(\sigma)
, for a surjective homomorphism
q\colon A\twoheadrightarrow B
, an embedding
e\colon C\rightarrowtail D
and morphisms
f,g
making the following diagram commute

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}

there exists a (necessarily unique) morphism
d\colon B\to C
such that
f=d\circ q
and
g=e\circ d
.

Claim proof.
For
b\in B
define
d(b)
as
f(a)
for an arbitrary
a\in A
such that
q(a)=b
. Such
a
exists because
q
is surjective. Moreover, the choice of
a
does not matter because if
q(a)=q(a^{\prime})=b
then
e(f(a))=g(q(a))=g(q(a^{\prime}))=e(f(a^{\prime}))
and since
e
is an embedding
f(a)=f(a^{\prime})
.

Observe that, by definition of
d
, we obtain the first desired equation, that is,
f=d\circ q
. The second one follows from the fact that
q
is surjective. Indeed, let
b\in B
. To show that
g(b)=e(d(b))
we take an arbitrary
a\in A
such
f(a)=b
. Then,
g(b)=g(q(a))=e(f(a))=e(d(q(a)))=e(d(b))
.

What remains to be verified is that
d
is a
\sigma
-structure homomorphism, which follows by similar reasoning to the above. Given an
n
-ary
R\in\sigma
, and
\overline{a}\in A^{n}
such that
R^{B}(q(\overline{a}))
, because
g
is a homomorphism also
R^{D}(g(q(\overline{a})))
or equivalently
R^{D}(e(f(\overline{a})))
. Finally, because
e
is an embedding, it reflects relations and so
R^{C}(f(\overline{a}))
, as we needed to prove.

\blacksquare

The fact that
(f[A],\beta^{\prime})
is a
\mathbb{C}
-coalgebra follows by a simple diagram chasing from the fact that
\varepsilon
and
\delta
are natural and that
q
is surjective.
∎

The proof of the following proceeds by checking the assumptions of Propositions
V.12
and
V.13
.

Proposition E.6
.

If
\mathbb{C}
preserves embeddings, and for any surjective morphism of coalgebras in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})

(A,\alpha)\to(B,\beta)

if
(A,\alpha)
is a path then so is
(B,\beta)
, then the lifting of
H(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}})=\prod_{i}A_{i}
satisfies axioms
(S1)
and
(S2)
.

Proof.

(S1)
For simplicity we only show that the binary product operation
H(A,A^{\prime})=A\times A^{\prime}
preserves embeddings on
\sigma
-structures where
\sigma
consists of one binary relation
R(\cdot,\cdot)
. Let
e\colon A\rightarrowtail B
and
e^{\prime}\colon A^{\prime}\rightarrowtail B^{\prime}
be two embeddings of
\sigma
-structures. The mapping
e\times e^{\prime}\colon A\times A^{\prime}\to B\times B^{\prime}
is injective since
e
and
e^{\prime}
are injective too. Moreover, it also reflect relations. Assume

R^{B\times B^{\prime}}(\left<e(x),e^{\prime}(x^{\prime})\right>,\ \left<e(y),e^{\prime}(y^{\prime})\right>)

which is equivalent to
R^{B}(e(x),e(y))
and
R^{B^{\prime}}(e^{\prime}(x^{\prime}),e^{\prime}(y^{\prime}))
. Because
e
and
e^{\prime}
are embeddings, we have that
R^{A}(x,y)
and
R^{A^{\prime}}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
which, in turn, is equivalent to

R^{A\times A^{\prime}}(\left<x,x^{\prime}\right>,\ \left<y,y^{\prime}\right>).

(S2)
We assume that
(Q,\rho)
is a path in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
and
(A_{i},\alpha_{i})
are arbitrary
\mathbb{C}
-coalgebras. Further, assume that we are given a morphism
f\colon P\to\prod_{i}A_{i}
which makes the following diagram commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(Q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(\prod_{i}A_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}(A_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}\alpha_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}

It is an easy observation that the composition
\pi_{i}\circ f
is a coalgebra morphism
(Q,\rho)\to(A_{i},\alpha_{i})
. By Lemma
E.4
, we can take a factorisation of this morphism as

(Q,\rho)\xrightarrow{q_{i}}(Q_{i},\rho_{i})\xrightarrow{e_{i}}(A_{i},\alpha_{i})

where
e_{i}
is an embedding. Since
(Q,\rho)
is a path and
q_{i}
is surjective, we see that
(Q_{i},\rho_{i})
is a path and, furthermore, we also know that
e_{i}
is an embedding. Consequently, we have a decomposition of
f
into

\textstyle Q\xrightarrow{q}\prod_{i}Q_{i}\xrightarrow{\prod_{i}e_{i}}\prod_{i}A_{i}

(15)

where
q
is obtained from the universal property of products, from the collection of morphisms
\{q_{i}\}_{i}
.

To show minimality of this decomposition, let
f
also decompose as

\textstyle Q\xrightarrow{r}\prod_{i}S_{i}\xrightarrow{\prod_{i}m_{i}}\prod_{i}A_{i}

(16)

where
m_{i}
are path embeddings
(S_{i},\omega_{i})\rightarrowtail(A_{i},\alpha_{i})
in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
. By post-composing both homomorphisms in (
15
) and (
16
) with the projection
\pi_{i}\colon\prod_{i}A_{i}\to A_{i}
we obtain a commutative square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{i}\circ r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{i}}

Now, observe that
q_{i}
is a surjective homomorphism and
m_{i}
is an embedding and so, by Claim
E.5
, there is a diagonal morphism
d_{i}\colon Q_{i}\to S_{i}
such that
e_{i}=m_{i}\circ d_{i}
. It remains to check is that
d_{i}
is a morphism of coalgebras
(Q_{i},\rho_{i})\to(S_{i},\omega_{i})
. This is a consequence of the fact that
\mathbb{C}(m_{i})
is an embedding and that the right square and the surrounding rectangle of the following diagram commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(Q_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(S_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(A_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(d_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(m_{i})}

Indeed, let
x\in Q_{i}
. Then, by chasing the above diagram we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(m_{i})(\omega_{i}(d_{i}(x)))=\alpha_{i}(m_{i}(d_{i}(x)))=\mathbb{C}(m_{i})(\mathbb{C}(d_{i})(\rho_{i}(x)))

and since
\mathbb{C}(m_{i})
is injective
\omega_{i}(d_{i}(x))=\mathbb{C}(d_{i})(\rho_{i}(x))
, as required.
∎

As a corollary of the above and Theorem
V.14
, we obtain a general FVM theorem for products, with assumptions that hold true for all the game comonads studied so far in the literature.

Theorem E.7
.

If
\mathbb{C}
preserves embeddings, and for any surjective morphism of coalgebras in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})

(A,\alpha)\to(B,\beta)

if
(A,\alpha)
is a path then so is
(B,\beta)
, then

\left(\forall i\in I.\;A_{i}\equiv_{\mathbb{C}}B_{i}\right)\quad\mbox{implies}\quad\prod_{i}A_{i}\equiv_{\mathbb{C}}\prod_{i}B_{i}.

Appendix F
Proofs omitted from Section
VII

See
VII.1

Proof.

Assume that
A_{i}\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{i}}B_{i}
, for every
i=1,\dots,n
.
By our assumptions, we obtain that
\mathfrak{t}_{i}(A_{i})\equiv_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{i}}\mathfrak{t}_{i}(B_{i})
for every
i=1,\dots,n
and, therefore,

H^{\prime}\left(\mathfrak{t}_{1}(A_{1}),\dots,\mathfrak{t}_{n}(A_{n})\right)\equiv_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{n+1}}H^{\prime}\left(\mathfrak{t}_{1}(B_{1}),\dots,\mathfrak{t}_{n}(B_{n})\right)

Then, by a straightforward calculation

\displaystyle\mathfrak{t}_{n+1}\left(H\left(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern
